Okay. Reason number&nbsp; three why you might not be able to regulate your&nbsp;&nbsp; nervous system is you're forcing it. You're&nbsp; trying to practice willingness in a secret&nbsp;&nbsp; attempt to make your feelings go away. You're&nbsp; practicing deep breathing in an attempt to force&nbsp;&nbsp; yourself and your body to calm down. Now, I talk&nbsp; about this cycle in my three videos that I made&nbsp;&nbsp; about panic attacks. The main idea is that when&nbsp; you have a physiological sensation and you say,&nbsp;&nbsp; "It's not okay for me to feel this regulated. I'm&nbsp; not okay. It's not acceptable if I feel stressed.&nbsp;&nbsp; It's not okay for me to have a fast heart rate.&nbsp; This is dangerous if I'm breathing fast." Right?&nbsp;&nbsp; "I have to make my breathing slow down." All of&nbsp; these thoughts send the message to your brain&nbsp;&nbsp; that you're in danger, that these sensations are&nbsp; dangerous. And if if you send that message to your&nbsp;&nbsp; brain that "Oh, I can't handle feeling this way,"&nbsp; that's going to accentuate the cycle of being&nbsp;&nbsp; dysregulated. So you feel stressed, and then&nbsp; you beat yourself up for feeling stressed, and&nbsp;&nbsp; that makes you more stressed. Now, the antidote to&nbsp; this is to allow yourself to feel; to let yourself&nbsp;&nbsp; feel uncomfortable, uncertain, vulnerable; to let&nbsp; yourself stand where you're standing; to be where&nbsp;&nbsp; you're at. It doesn't mean that you're going to&nbsp; feel this way forever, but you have to start with&nbsp;&nbsp; where you're at. So true willingness to feel your&nbsp; emotions means that your default setting is to use&nbsp;&nbsp; acceptance as a launching point for change, not&nbsp; the other way around, not saying, "After I change,&nbsp;&nbsp; then I can allow myself to feel." And people have&nbsp; this tendency when things are really painful to&nbsp;&nbsp; try to speed up, to make it pass faster. But&nbsp; with emotions, trying to hurry actually prevents&nbsp;&nbsp; you from doing the one thing that does work,&nbsp; which is slowing down and sitting with them.&nbsp;&nbsp; So just to summarize: If the problem is that you&nbsp; just need to build up your parasympathetic muscle,&nbsp;&nbsp; set a goal to practice body-based nervous system&nbsp; regulation a few extra minutes a day. If there's&nbsp;&nbsp; actually a problem that needs to be resolved, take&nbsp; some action. And if you're forcing it, it's time&nbsp;&nbsp; to give yourself just a ton of compassion around&nbsp; being dysregulated. Slow down and create some&nbsp;&nbsp; space for you to feel what you feel without trying&nbsp; to force it to go away. With practice and the&nbsp;&nbsp; right approach, you really can unlock your nervous&nbsp; system, strengthen your parasympathetic response,&nbsp;&nbsp; and foster a greater sense of safety and calm. I&nbsp; hope this video helps you get better at feeling.&nbsp;&nbsp; Thank you for watching, and take care. Oh, and&nbsp; as a side note, if you'd like to buy this shirt,&nbsp;&nbsp; my friend Nikki has made a um shirt company,&nbsp;&nbsp; and 50% of the proceeds go to helping teens get&nbsp; therapy services and outdoor experiences that&nbsp;&nbsp; they wouldn't otherwise be able to access. So&nbsp; check it out in the merch shelf below. [Music]

  11. @ScottKiloby: Nervous System Regulation v. True Healing and Embodiment 

There is a significant tension in the world of nervous system work between regulation and resolution. Most of what is taught today—breathwork, cold plunges, or rhythmic tapping—is designed to move the needle from a "hot," frantic state back to a "cool," functional state. This is surface regulation. It is a vital survival skill, but if it’s used to bypass the "pinched" sensations of childhood repression, it becomes just another sophisticated way to escape feeling scary stuff. 

In the landscape of recovery, there are very few exceptions to this rule. 

However, a few specific approaches attempt to bridge the gap:

The "Bottom-Up" Exceptions
The most notable exceptions are found in somatic modalities that refuse to view "activation" as something to be managed. In these spaces, a "heavy" chest or a "tight" throat isn't seen as a symptom to be regulated away, but as the actual doorway.

 * Somatic Experiencing: Developed by Peter Levine, this focuses on "titration"—taking a tiny drop of the "hot" repressed energy and letting the body process it without being overwhelmed. It isn't just about getting calm; it's about letting the body finally finish a survival reflex that was frozen decades ago.

 * The Kiloby Inquiries: This is perhaps the most direct exception. It uses a "Reverse Inquiry" to intentionally trigger the buried emotion so that it can be looked at. Instead of trying to feel better, the goal is to get better at feeling. By "firing" consciousness into the center of the contraction, the repression is unmasked rather than just calmed down.

The Trap of the "Regulated Ego"

Many people fall into a trap where they become "professional regulators." They have the perfect morning routine and the perfect breathing cadence, but their life still feels like a performance. Their nervous system is "quiet," but it isn't free. The "clench" is still there; it’s just been sedated by biohacking.

True resolution only happens when the system realizes it no longer needs to repress anything to be safe. You can't regulate your way into that realization. You have to go toward the "hot" zone, stay with the "raw data" of the buried emotion, and allow the body to realize that the old threat is over. The only real exception to surface regulation is the willingness to be dysregulated long enough to let the past finally burn off.

- Catastrophizing: How to Stop Making Yourself Depressed and Anxious: Cognitive Distortion Skill #6: Kind: captions Language: en Hi, everyone. I'm Emma McAdam. I'm a licensed&nbsp; marriage and family therapist. And in this&nbsp;&nbsp; video we're going to talk about catastrophizing,&nbsp; which is expecting the worst. Catastrophizing&nbsp;&nbsp; is an excellent way to make yourself anxious and&nbsp; depressed and completely ruin your life. Oh, yeah,&nbsp;&nbsp; and I'm going to teach you three things you can do&nbsp; to stop catastrophizing. So let's talk about that. Late one night a man was driving along a dark&nbsp; rural road. He was out far from any towns,&nbsp;&nbsp; and he was just trying to get home. All of&nbsp; a sudden he hears a loud bang and the thump&nbsp;&nbsp; thump thump of a flat tire. He's irritated, but no&nbsp; need to panic; he knows how to change a tire. So&nbsp;&nbsp; he gets out of the car, he gets into the trunk, he&nbsp; pulls out the spare tire and the lug wrench. And&nbsp;&nbsp; then he realizes with a sinking feeling that his&nbsp; jack is missing. You can't change a tire without a&nbsp;&nbsp; jack. Then he checks his phone and he doesn't have&nbsp; reception. So now he's stuck. As he's wondering&nbsp;&nbsp; what to do, he looks down the road and he sees&nbsp; a porch light a long ways away. It looks like a&nbsp;&nbsp; little farmhouse. And he decides to walk over and&nbsp; ask the farmer if he could borrow a jack. The walk&nbsp;&nbsp; was long and dark. And at one point he trips over&nbsp; a pothole and he falls and he tears a hole in his&nbsp;&nbsp; pants. And he just he keeps stumbling along in&nbsp; the dark, worrying about what he's going to do.&nbsp;&nbsp; It's dark, it's late, and he starts to imagine&nbsp; what will happen when he reaches the farmhouse.&nbsp;&nbsp; The farmer will probably already be in bed.&nbsp; He'll probably be cranky about being woken up.&nbsp;&nbsp; But the man keeps walking. And as he walks through&nbsp; the dark, other thoughts come to him. What if the&nbsp;&nbsp; farmer doesn't have a jack? Farmers have guns.&nbsp; What if he pulls that out? The farmer's probably&nbsp;&nbsp; going to sic a huge dog on him. What if the farmer&nbsp; realizes that the man's alone and he robs him?&nbsp;&nbsp; He thinks, "I don't deserve to be treated that&nbsp; way. None of this is fair." And at this point&nbsp;&nbsp; the man is scared, but he's also getting angry.&nbsp; He knows that this farmer is going to be a jerk,&nbsp;&nbsp; but he still needs his jack. So he walks up to&nbsp; the front door and he knocks. And as he waits&nbsp;&nbsp; for a response, he imagines this angry farmer&nbsp; screaming at him, threatening him, chasing him,&nbsp;&nbsp; shooting him. And he hears footsteps. The door&nbsp; swings open, and a man says, "Can I help you?"&nbsp;&nbsp; And the stranded man, all red-faced and&nbsp; angry, yells, "I don't want your dang&nbsp;&nbsp; jack anyway!" and he grabs the door and&nbsp; he slams it shut and he storms away. Now,&nbsp;&nbsp; catastrophizing is a common cognitive distortion&nbsp; or thinking error. It's when we think of a current&nbsp;&nbsp; or future situation as a catastrophe. So for&nbsp; example, you worry that you're gonna fail a test,&nbsp;&nbsp; and then you imagine what would happen when you&nbsp; do fail. You're gonna get kicked out of school.&nbsp;&nbsp; You're going to end up working a dead-end job,&nbsp; fail at life, and die homeless on the street.&nbsp;&nbsp; And all of this because of a test at school.&nbsp; Catastrophizing is imagining the worst.&nbsp;&nbsp; It's taking a difficult situation and&nbsp; interpreting it as being horrible, terrible,&nbsp;&nbsp; and unrecoverable. We all know that person who if&nbsp; they get a B on a test wails, "I'm failing math."&nbsp;&nbsp; Right? Many of us have had a parent who when&nbsp; we didn't want to do our chores, they said&nbsp;&nbsp; something like, "Oh, if you don't do your chores,&nbsp; your college roommates will hate you and no one&nbsp;&nbsp; will want to marry you." Right? Like in the story&nbsp; about the jack, catastrophizing often starts with&nbsp;&nbsp; a genuine setback, like getting a flat tire in&nbsp; the middle of nowhere. But then the thinking error&nbsp;&nbsp; turns that reality into a belief that something&nbsp; horrible is likely to happen. "I'm gonna get shot&nbsp;&nbsp; or robbed and attacked." Right? At its root,&nbsp; catastrophizing is about our habitual response&nbsp;&nbsp; to challenges or shortcomings. So take a second&nbsp; pause, this video, and ask yourself how do you&nbsp;&nbsp; think about failure? When these habits, these&nbsp; thinking habits become part of a repeated pattern,&nbsp;&nbsp; they lead to depression or anxiety, and people&nbsp; tend to imagine that they'll never be able&nbsp;&nbsp; to recover. So here's some common examples.&nbsp; Someone with anxiety imagines losing control&nbsp;&nbsp; of himself. So for example, a man with a panic&nbsp; disorder predicts that if he goes to the mall&nbsp;&nbsp; on a weekend afternoon, he'll have a panic attack&nbsp; ,and then he predicts that having a panic attack&nbsp;&nbsp; would be a catastrophe rather than&nbsp; just being really uncomfortable.&nbsp;&nbsp; Or a woman with depression envisions herself being&nbsp; depressed forever and never feeling happy again.&nbsp;&nbsp; Or a 30-year-old man imagines himself never&nbsp; finding love and imagining that if he doesn't,&nbsp;&nbsp; he will be plagued by intense feelings of&nbsp; loneliness 24/7 from now until he dies.&nbsp;&nbsp; Right? Or a teen equates not being in a text&nbsp; group with being totally rejected by everyone.&nbsp;&nbsp; So how does catastrophizing mess us up? We have&nbsp; all experienced some tragedies in our life,&nbsp;&nbsp; including painful rejection or failure, and I&nbsp; think that we trick ourselves into believing&nbsp;&nbsp; that if we can expect the worst we can prevent&nbsp; it. But in reality, usually the exact opposite&nbsp;&nbsp; happens. So think about the man from the jack&nbsp; story. Because he feared getting rejected,&nbsp;&nbsp; he slammed the door shut himself. He cut&nbsp; himself off from the opportunity to get&nbsp;&nbsp; the solution he needed because he was thinking&nbsp; about everything that could go wrong. Seeing&nbsp;&nbsp; the worst often invites the worst. Not only&nbsp; do we cut ourselves off from opportunities,&nbsp;&nbsp; but we invite the exact problems we're hoping to&nbsp; avoid. So if we go into a conversation expecting&nbsp;&nbsp; the other person to get defensive, we often lead&nbsp; off by being harsher or more rigid, inviting the&nbsp;&nbsp; other to be more defensive. If you expect that&nbsp; your crush will reject you if you ask her out,&nbsp;&nbsp; then you don't ask her out. You end up alone on&nbsp; the weekend. Catastrophizing invites depression.&nbsp;&nbsp; When we imagine a future that is bleak,&nbsp; threatening, or hopeless, then our brain&nbsp;&nbsp; responds by putting out less serotonin and&nbsp; dopamine. These are the happiness, pleasure,&nbsp;&nbsp; and motivation chemicals. So why be happy or&nbsp; hopeful when the future is impossibly dreary?&nbsp;&nbsp; This leads to a cycle of withdrawal from life, a&nbsp; lack of motivation, and a pattern of depression.&nbsp;&nbsp; Catastrophizing also invites anxiety. It&nbsp; forces our brain to see threats and failure&nbsp;&nbsp; everywhere. And our brain responds to perceived&nbsp; threats with a very real physical fear response,&nbsp;&nbsp; the fight/flight/freeze response. And this&nbsp; contributes to social anxiety, general anxiety,&nbsp;&nbsp; panic attacks, and more. Expecting the&nbsp; worst makes us hopeless and depressed&nbsp;&nbsp; about the future. It makes us unmotivated. Why&nbsp; try if I'm just gonna fail? And it also enables&nbsp;&nbsp; us to wallow in self-pity. Catastrophizing&nbsp; closes us off to opportunities and options&nbsp;&nbsp; that might work, and it leads to this sense of&nbsp; paralysis. So if catastrophizing is so harmful,&nbsp;&nbsp; why do we keep doing it? At this point I've&nbsp; got to pause because some of you out there&nbsp;&nbsp; have started this super unhelpful thought process&nbsp; of "Yeah, why am I such an idiot? I am so broken.&nbsp;&nbsp; See, I am defective because I do this stuff."&nbsp; Okay. You need to stop that. Take a deep breath.&nbsp;&nbsp; You are not defective. You might be doing&nbsp; something that's not working well for you,&nbsp;&nbsp; but that doesn't mean you're bad or broken. It&nbsp; means you can change and get feeling better.&nbsp;&nbsp; So if you need to, pause this video and take a&nbsp; second to be kind to yourself and practice some&nbsp;&nbsp; courage. Changing how we think takes work, but you&nbsp; can do hard things. Okay. So let's go back to the&nbsp;&nbsp; question, why do we catastrophize? Well, it serves&nbsp; a function, albeit a dysfunctional one. We do it&nbsp;&nbsp; for one of two reasons. Number one: preparing&nbsp; for the worst is a coping strategy, preventing us&nbsp;&nbsp; from feeling risk or uncertainty. So if I expect&nbsp; myself to fail, I won't be disappointed if I do.&nbsp;&nbsp; If I reject myself first, then I don't have&nbsp; to worry that my crush will do it to me.&nbsp;&nbsp; Catastrophizing is an attempt to avoid feelings,&nbsp; to protect ourselves from feeling sadness or&nbsp;&nbsp; worry. But the crazy thing is that when we try&nbsp; not to feel, we often end up more depressed and&nbsp;&nbsp; anxious. So expecting the worst also justifies us&nbsp; for not even trying, and it attempts to excuse our&nbsp;&nbsp; failure before we put in any effort. So no wonder&nbsp; it feels more comfortable than putting your heart&nbsp;&nbsp; out there. It's comfortable in the short term, but&nbsp; it crushes the joy out of life in the long run.&nbsp;&nbsp; When you catastrophize, you're not risking&nbsp; failure, but you can't have success. You're not&nbsp;&nbsp; getting rejected, but you're still alone on the&nbsp; weekend. Okay. Dysfunctional function number two:&nbsp;&nbsp; sometimes we think or we've been trained to&nbsp; believe that the best motivation is fear,&nbsp;&nbsp; that in order to motivate ourselves to study or to&nbsp; go to work we have to predict doom and gloom. Fear&nbsp;&nbsp; as motivation works briefly, but in the long run&nbsp; it makes us depressed and anxious and overwhelmed&nbsp;&nbsp; and less functional. Let me use a school anxiety&nbsp; example. So a kid's not going to school because of&nbsp;&nbsp; anxiety. The parents also feel anxious about this,&nbsp; so they go into a room and say, "You have to get&nbsp;&nbsp; up or else you are going to ruin your life. You&nbsp; have to go to school or you'll never get a good&nbsp;&nbsp; job," etc., etc. And in the short term this gets&nbsp; the kid out of bed and into school. But then she&nbsp;&nbsp; spends the rest of her day worrying about being&nbsp; a failure, and the next day she has even more&nbsp;&nbsp; anxiety and it's even harder to get motivated&nbsp; to go to school. So do you do this? Do you&nbsp;&nbsp; try to give yourself a pep talk but it's really&nbsp; more of a fear talk? We or our parents may have&nbsp;&nbsp; used fear in the past as a strong motivator, but&nbsp; it's just not a sustainable source of motivation.&nbsp;&nbsp; So let's find something that's more functional&nbsp; than our self-justifying, self-defeating&nbsp;&nbsp; catastrophizing. So first off, try to start with&nbsp; a good night's rest. When we're sleep-deprived,&nbsp;&nbsp; we're hypersensitive to threats and we're less&nbsp; resilient in the face of challenges. When you're&nbsp;&nbsp; rested you'll have a greater ability to face&nbsp; these challenges bravely. Okay. Number two: accept&nbsp;&nbsp; uncertainty as a natural and acceptable part of&nbsp; living a wholehearted life. This is a fundamental&nbsp;&nbsp; life skill that can be developed and practiced.&nbsp; It involves changing how you think about anxiety.&nbsp;&nbsp; So instead of labeling anxiety as bad or harmful&nbsp; or terrible or "I can't handle it," you say,&nbsp;&nbsp; "Well, this is uncomfortable, but it won't&nbsp; injure me." You say, "I can do hard things."&nbsp;&nbsp; Courage is not the absence of fear but the&nbsp; judgment that something else is more important.&nbsp;&nbsp; So living life is about embracing acceptable risk&nbsp; and the anxiety that comes with it as a normal and&nbsp;&nbsp; natural and helpful part of life. And as you do&nbsp; this, you build up emotional muscles to experience&nbsp;&nbsp; uncomfortable emotions. And you can also practice&nbsp; this by doing mindfulness and meditation or just&nbsp;&nbsp; doing something every day that scares you. Okay.&nbsp; Number three: motivate yourself by what you want&nbsp;&nbsp; in life, by what you value and you hope instead of&nbsp; trying to use fear. So these are called positive&nbsp;&nbsp; goals. So instead of saying, you know, "Oh, I have&nbsp; to go to school so that I don't die homeless on&nbsp;&nbsp; the street," you say, "I choose to go to school&nbsp; because I want to be a therapist when I grow up."&nbsp;&nbsp; Okay. I never said that as a kid, but you get&nbsp; the idea. Right? Choose what you do want in&nbsp;&nbsp; life. Break it down into small goals, and then&nbsp; just bravely work toward those little by little.&nbsp;&nbsp; Okay. So here's the classic CBT approach to ending&nbsp; catastrophizing. So number one: just start by&nbsp;&nbsp; noticing when you are catastrophizing. What are&nbsp; the words you use? Are they things like never,&nbsp;&nbsp; terrible, fail, rejected, awkward? Anything that's&nbsp; an exaggeration, making things out to be worse&nbsp;&nbsp; than they are. And then getting better as well&nbsp; at noticing what are the situations you tend to&nbsp;&nbsp; catastrophize about? And, you know, write down&nbsp; what it looks like when you do it. Ask a family&nbsp;&nbsp; member or a friend to point it out to you. Right?&nbsp; So this is the first step in emotion processing.&nbsp;&nbsp; It's observing, it's getting better at noticing&nbsp; what's going on with your thought patterns and&nbsp;&nbsp; your behaviors. Then the second step in emotion&nbsp; processing is to pause. Right? So just because&nbsp;&nbsp; you think something doesn't mean it's true. Just&nbsp; because you feel something doesn't mean you have&nbsp;&nbsp; to believe it. So now is a great chance. Just&nbsp; slow it down. Take a deep breath. Number three is&nbsp;&nbsp; explore. Right? Challenge those thoughts. Right?&nbsp; Just because you think something doesn't mean it's&nbsp;&nbsp; true. Learn to notice and gently question your&nbsp; thoughts. You don't have to believe everything you&nbsp;&nbsp; think, but you also don't need to beat yourself&nbsp; up for having these thoughts, like saying things&nbsp;&nbsp; like, "Oh, what's the matter with me? Why do&nbsp; I always think this way?" That's also not very&nbsp;&nbsp; helpful. So instead, like, get better at noticing&nbsp; your thoughts and letting them pass through. This&nbsp;&nbsp; is another skill from acceptance and commitment&nbsp; therapy. It's called cognitive diffusion, and&nbsp;&nbsp; you can practice it with activities like Leaves&nbsp; on the Stream or the skills I teach in my video How to Stop Intrusive Thoughts and&nbsp; Overthinking. So I'll link those below.&nbsp;&nbsp; Okay. Number four is choose. Right? Replace those&nbsp; thoughts with something more honest and more&nbsp;&nbsp; helpful. So once you start to notice this type&nbsp; of thinking, you pause and you explore it. You&nbsp;&nbsp; can bravely pick up your emotional sword, and&nbsp; you can begin to combat this kind of thinking&nbsp;&nbsp; with more honest and more rational thoughts,&nbsp; thoughts that line up more with who you want to&nbsp;&nbsp; be and your values. And some of the ways I like to&nbsp; do this are to think things like this: "Okay, even&nbsp;&nbsp; if something bad did happen, I could learn from&nbsp; it. It wouldn't be the end of the world," etc.&nbsp;&nbsp; So here's a couple of examples. Say someone&nbsp; says something like, "Oh no, I am such an idiot.&nbsp;&nbsp; I already made a mistake on this report;&nbsp; I'm never gonna finish it. Or if I do,&nbsp;&nbsp; it's gonna be so flawed that it won't matter.&nbsp; I'm gonna get fired no matter what." Okay. Wait.&nbsp;&nbsp; Pause. That's not true. Okay. Let's explore some&nbsp; alternatives here. "Everybody makes mistakes." Uh&nbsp;&nbsp; okay. "I'm only human." There's another one.&nbsp; "I'll fix this mistake, and if I need to ask&nbsp;&nbsp; for help I can. But I'm just gonna keep working&nbsp; hard and try to be more careful in the future."&nbsp;&nbsp; Or something more like, "Oh, nobody's gonna&nbsp; fire me for a mistake or two in a report."&nbsp;&nbsp; Okay. See how we're replacing the catastrophizing&nbsp; thoughts with thoughts that are a little bit more&nbsp;&nbsp; helpful? Okay. Here's another example: someone&nbsp; says something like, "Oh, I can't believe I said&nbsp;&nbsp; that to my boyfriend. He's gonna leave me for&nbsp; sure this time." Okay. Let's replace that with&nbsp;&nbsp; maybe like, "Oh, I shouldn't have said that to&nbsp; my boyfriend. I really need to learn how to talk&nbsp;&nbsp; kindly when I'm upset. I'm gonna apologize and try&nbsp; to make it right. Hopefully he'll understand and&nbsp;&nbsp; accept my apology and we'll both learn something&nbsp; from this." Okay. So challenging catastrophizing,&nbsp;&nbsp; this approach, it requires us to stay engaged even&nbsp; when there's a risk of things not going perfectly.&nbsp;&nbsp; And this is called vulnerability, the potential&nbsp; for success and also for getting hurt. But the&nbsp;&nbsp; only alternative is to guarantee failure by&nbsp; cutting yourself off before you even try.&nbsp;&nbsp; So I'm a big fan of acceptance and commitment&nbsp; therapy. It's it's a process which basically&nbsp;&nbsp; trains you to get better at feeling, to&nbsp; open yourself up to the emotions that come&nbsp;&nbsp; with living the life you value - love, joy&nbsp; and sadness and worry and hope, excitement,&nbsp;&nbsp; anxiety. As you come to wholeheartedly&nbsp; embrace life and your goals and your values,&nbsp;&nbsp; you'll get better and better at living with some&nbsp; risk, and you'll be rewarded with lots of good&nbsp;&nbsp; things happening to you all the time. May good&nbsp; things come to you as you courageously face life&nbsp;&nbsp; and the risks, joys, loves that come with it. For&nbsp; those of you who are long-time followers, you may&nbsp;&nbsp; have noticed that this video is a remake of one&nbsp; of my old videos, and that's because that video&nbsp;&nbsp; had a couple things that needed to be improved. So&nbsp; I hope you don't mind me making a better version&nbsp;&nbsp; of my video on catastrophizing. So&nbsp; thank you all for watching. Take care.

But that thought, you know, 'Oh, I'm&nbsp; so awkward' is not super helpful to me right now,&nbsp;&nbsp; so I'm gonna go back to paying attention to&nbsp; listening to my friend." That's that's another&nbsp;&nbsp; one. That's another cognitive defusion technique.&nbsp; It's called thanking your mind. So you just say,&nbsp;&nbsp; "Thank you, mind, for that thought. That's&nbsp; a thought. Just because Ii think it doesn't&nbsp;&nbsp; mean it's true, doesn't mean I have to act&nbsp; on it, doesn't mean I have to believe it."&nbsp;&nbsp; Okay, here's another fun act exercise. Um they&nbsp; have hundreds of these, by the way, right. These&nbsp;&nbsp; are all thanks to Stephen Hayes and Jason Luoma&nbsp; and Russ Harris and all the other great act&nbsp;&nbsp; developers out there, Acceptance and Commitment&nbsp; therapy. So here's another one: using singing&nbsp;&nbsp; and silly voices, right. So you take the thought&nbsp; that seems really strong like "Oh, I can't go to&nbsp;&nbsp; that party if I feel anxious." And then you start&nbsp; to use weird voices to say that thought out loud:&nbsp;&nbsp; I can't go to that party if I feel anxious.&nbsp; I can't go to that party if I feel anxious.&nbsp;&nbsp; I can't go to that party if I feel anxious. Okay,&nbsp; I'm terrible at accents, so I should not even try,&nbsp;&nbsp; right. I can't go to that party if I feel anxious.&nbsp; And the basic idea is that when you start to say&nbsp;&nbsp; a thought in a bunch of weird ways, it starts&nbsp; to feel like this weird thing, like this weird&nbsp;&nbsp; jumble of words, instead of feel like just,&nbsp; you know, the water that you're swimming in.&nbsp;&nbsp; Same thing happens if you say a word over and&nbsp; over again. So if, for example, Miguel is like,&nbsp;&nbsp; "Oh my gosh, I'm so awkward," then he says the&nbsp; word "awkward" over and over and over again:&nbsp;&nbsp; awkward awkward awkward awkward awkward - say it&nbsp; with me - awkward awkward awkward awkward awkward&nbsp;&nbsp; awkward awkward awkward awkward awkward awkward&nbsp; awkward awkward awkward. And at some point,&nbsp;&nbsp; awkward all of a sudden turns into this weird&nbsp; jumble of sounds. That's a cognitive defusion&nbsp;&nbsp; technique, right. This is just a word. Your brain&nbsp; is a word machine, and everything it makes up,&nbsp;&nbsp; some of it's true, some of it isn't. Some of it's&nbsp; helpful, some of it's not. It's just a thought.&nbsp;&nbsp; And when we separate ourselves from our thoughts,&nbsp; we can create choice for ourselves. Okay,&nbsp;&nbsp; another exercise that's often helpful is to&nbsp; symbolically put your thoughts onto an object&nbsp;&nbsp; to give them a tangible form. The easiest way to&nbsp; do this is to write. Write it down on a piece of&nbsp;&nbsp; paper. Bruce Lee said, "I have a system of ridding&nbsp; my mind of negative thoughts - I visualize myself&nbsp;&nbsp; writing them down on a piece of paper. Then&nbsp; I imagine myself crumpling up the paper,&nbsp;&nbsp; lighting it on fire, and burning it to a crisp."&nbsp; In residential treatment, I worked with a whole&nbsp;&nbsp; bunch of kids who found it helpful to make their&nbsp; thoughts concrete by putting them on an object.&nbsp;&nbsp; So I had one client who had really low self-esteem&nbsp; - and again, we were not forcing the kids to do&nbsp;&nbsp; this; they were choosing to uh engage in these uh&nbsp; activities to help themselves. And it wasn't about&nbsp;&nbsp; shame or like the scarlet letter or anything&nbsp; like that. So she, this woman, this this young&nbsp;&nbsp; lady with low self-esteem, she decided to get a&nbsp; huge rock, like, you know, like a 10-pound rock.&nbsp;&nbsp; And she wrote on it "I'm unlovable." And when&nbsp; she believed that thought, when she bought&nbsp;&nbsp; that thought, she would carry that rock around.&nbsp; And when she chose not to believe that thought,&nbsp;&nbsp; when she chose not to buy that thought, she would&nbsp; set that rock down. So she made a physical way&nbsp;&nbsp; to represent this idea of having a thought versus&nbsp; buying a thought. Like that rock might have still&nbsp;&nbsp; been in the room and she might still be noticing&nbsp; that rock, but just because she was noticing it&nbsp;&nbsp; didn't mean that she believed it. Lots of thoughts&nbsp; are going to pop up in your head throughout the&nbsp;&nbsp; day. Average people have intrusive thoughts&nbsp; all the time. We all might have inappropriate&nbsp;&nbsp; thoughts or negative thoughts or true thoughts&nbsp; or false thoughts. It's okay to have a thought;&nbsp;&nbsp; it doesn't say anything about you. Your brain&nbsp; is a word machine. But if you're going to buy a&nbsp;&nbsp; thought, if you're going to believe it and&nbsp; act on it, make that a conscious choice.&nbsp;&nbsp; One of the ways to do this is to just put it&nbsp; on an object, right. I've had clients who have&nbsp;&nbsp; chosen to carry rocks, sticks, and even horse poop&nbsp; in a bag as a way to represent themselves being&nbsp;&nbsp; fused with their thoughts. And when they're ready&nbsp; to notice them and separate from them, they would&nbsp;&nbsp; set these objects aside. When they no longer need&nbsp; that thought, they let go and go of an object.&nbsp;&nbsp; Sometimes they'll come back to it, but&nbsp; gradually just practicing, you know,&nbsp;&nbsp; like labeling it and choosing it if they want to,&nbsp; that can help them kind of create that separation.&nbsp;&nbsp; Cognitive defusion gives you the freedom to ask,&nbsp; "Does buying this thought make my life better?"&nbsp;&nbsp; That's what act is all about: it's freeing us to&nbsp; live the life we value instead of getting sucked&nbsp;&nbsp; into thoughts. So you say, "This is a thought.&nbsp; It's just a thought. I don't have to believe it,&nbsp;&nbsp; I don't have to act on it, I don't have&nbsp; to fight it. I can let it pass through."&nbsp;&nbsp; So the essential question is "Is this thought&nbsp; helpful to me? Does it help me live my values?"&nbsp;&nbsp; If the thought is helpful, you can buy it, you&nbsp; can believe it, you can hang on to it, you can&nbsp;&nbsp; act on it. And if it's not helpful, then you can&nbsp; notice it. You can just have it but not buy it.&nbsp;&nbsp; It's just a thought. Thoughts pop up all the time,&nbsp; and not all of them are helpful. So for example,&nbsp;&nbsp; fusion with thoughts says, "Oh, I have to stop&nbsp; being anxious if I want to go to parties."&nbsp;&nbsp; Or with OCD it says, "Oh, I have to make this&nbsp; thought about washing my hands go away." It traps&nbsp;&nbsp; us in a cycle of fighting our thoughts or just&nbsp; believing them without even noticing it, right.&nbsp;&nbsp; With cognitive defusion, we create space between&nbsp; ourselves and our thoughts and feelings so that&nbsp;&nbsp; they can have less of a hold over us. So you can&nbsp; say, "I can feel anxious and go to that party. I&nbsp;&nbsp; can have the thought that I need to wash my hands&nbsp; 20 times, but I don't have to buy that thought."&nbsp;&nbsp; Now let's just take a minute and contrast this&nbsp; with CBT, cognitive behavioral therapy, right. So&nbsp;&nbsp; in section 18 and 19, we talked about challenging&nbsp; distorted thinking. This is a CBT skill.&nbsp;&nbsp; So you might take the thought like, "Oh, I'm a&nbsp; complete loser," and then you would challenge&nbsp;&nbsp; that thought. You would say, "Oh, that's black&nbsp; and white thinking. Let's look for exceptions.&nbsp;&nbsp; What would a kind friend say about that?" CBT&nbsp; gives you the skill of challenging your thoughts,&nbsp;&nbsp; which is a helpful skill for some people or&nbsp; in some situations. Now for other people,&nbsp;&nbsp; that leads them to constantly struggling against&nbsp; their thoughts. So with Acceptance and Commitment&nbsp;&nbsp; therapy, we add the skill of defusion. It's the&nbsp; ability to separate yourself from your thoughts&nbsp;&nbsp; and to choose your actions. So you'd look at a&nbsp; thought like "I'm a complete loser" and you'd say,&nbsp;&nbsp; "Thanks, mind." And you say and then you'd&nbsp; ask, you know, "Is this thought helpful for me?&nbsp;&nbsp; Thank you, mind, for giving me this thought that&nbsp; I'm a complete loser, but this is just a thought.&nbsp;&nbsp; Is this thought helpful to me?" And if it's not&nbsp; helpful, if it doesn't help you live your values,&nbsp;&nbsp; then you just let that thought be there. You let&nbsp; that thought pass along, do whatever thoughts do.&nbsp;&nbsp; And you just look around for another&nbsp; thought that's more helpful to you.&nbsp;&nbsp; Now it's not that like cognitive defusion is&nbsp; better and challenging cognitive distortions&nbsp;&nbsp; is worse; it's just that these are different&nbsp; skills, different tools. When you add skills&nbsp;&nbsp; to your emotional toolbelt, you have more&nbsp; flexibility to find the one that works for you.&nbsp;&nbsp; So in summary, don't get obsessed&nbsp; with fighting your thoughts,&nbsp;&nbsp; with trying to make them go away or just&nbsp; worrying about them or reacting to them.&nbsp;&nbsp; You don't have to believe everything you think.&nbsp; With cognitive defusion, we create a little space&nbsp;&nbsp; between ourselves and our thoughts. We notice&nbsp; them. We say, "I'm having the thought that&nbsp;&nbsp; like I'm awkward or I'm a loser." Instead of&nbsp; saying, "I'm a loser," say "I'm having the thought&nbsp;&nbsp; that I'm a loser." Then we can ask ourselves,&nbsp; "Is this thought, is this thought helpful?&nbsp;&nbsp; Does it help me live the life I value?" So "Does&nbsp; that thought help me? Oh, I'm such a loser. Hmm.&nbsp;&nbsp; It doesn't really help me take any action.&nbsp; Okay, I'm just going to notice that thought.&nbsp;&nbsp; I'm going to choose one thing I can do to, I don't&nbsp; know, make my life better or whatever that is."&nbsp;&nbsp; So that frees us to choose what's most important&nbsp; and to allow other thoughts to pass through so&nbsp;&nbsp; that we can focus on living the life we want.&nbsp; The Acceptance and Commitment therapy gurus&nbsp;&nbsp; have made some great videos and recordings&nbsp; to help people understand and practice this,&nbsp;&nbsp; so check out a few of them. The links are below.&nbsp; Thank you for watching, and take care.   17. @JohnKlariti: This is such an important distinction.

Most people try to “fix” every negative thought, but sometimes that just turns into wrestling with your own brain in a tiny mental cage.

The real shift is learning to see the thought instead of becoming it.

“I’m not good enough” feels heavy when you believe it as truth.

But “I’m noticing the thought that I’m not good enough” creates space.

And in that space, you get choice.

That’s where everything changes.

You don’t need to obey every thought.
You don’t need to fight every thought.
You can notice it, question if it helps your values, and then choose the next clean step anyway.

I call this mental static.

The thought may still be loud… but loud doesn’t mean true.

- The Psychology Of Personal Space: Kind: captions Language: en Back in the 1970s, this guy was part of a psychological study... but the whole time he had no idea. He will never know. We don't know his name, but we do know... he needed to pee. One day, 60 unwitting men went into a public bathroom to do their business. For 20 of them, no one else was around and on average, from the time they reached the urinal it took them 4.8 seconds to start to pee. For the next 20 guys, one actor was standing one urinal away and on average, it took them 6.2 seconds to begin. For the final 20 guys, the actor stood unnervingly close and it took them an average of 8.4 seconds to start urinating. The study showed that an invasion of personal space led to a delayed onset of men's urination... causing a slow, fragmented piddle. The researchers were criticised for being unethical... they observed participants through a periscope hidden in a stack of books. And of course other things can lead to how we define our personal space - like your personality or culture. A recent study found that anxious people need a larger personal space. But why are we surrounded by these invisible bubbles of personal space? When other people get too close, we elicit a strong fear response. The amygdala, a region of the brain associated with fear, is activated... in case you get poked in the eye or something. One patient with lesions to the amygdala felt entirely comfortable no matter how close people got to her, and she seemed to have no personal space. For those with your amygdala in tact, you're generally cocooned by bubbles of four different sizes. Intimate space extends out an arms length from your body in every direction. It's reserved for your closest family, friends and pets. Personal space extends from an arms length to just over a metre away. Friends and acquaintances can enter, but strangers are a no-no. Social space extends from just over 1-2 metres from your body, where you interact with new acquaintances or total strangers. And public space is open to all. These bubbles were defined by American anthropologist Edward Hall - for American men. Perhaps they reign true for those in the land of the free, but a busy footpath in Hong Kong may just burst your bubble. Hall noted that in Latin America or the Middle East, people talk at distances that Americans would consider space invaders. Wherever you're walking or urinating in the world, if someone comes into your intimate or personal space, your brain does find it threatening. If you're shy, a space invasion is even more daunting - for a shy bladder, sometimes any audience causes stage fright. What do you think about personal space bubbles? Let me know what space invasions burst your bubble, and where you're from, in the comments. And subscribe to BrainCraft, for a new episode... soon to be every week! - Autism and Personal Space : Animated Social Stories for Children With Autism: Kind: captions Language: en [Music] so do you

- The Hidden TRUTH of Personal Space | Psychological Cultural & Social Dynamics: Kind: captions Language: en Imagine [Music] waiting in line at a coffee shop. You're minding your own business, scrolling through your phone when someone behind you inches forward, so close you can feel their breath. Instinctively, you shift, lean away, or take a step forward. That reaction isn't just social awkwardness. It's a psychological defense. Our brains are wired to protect our perpersonal space, the zone immediately surrounding our bodies because it's tied to survival, autonomy, and emotional regulation, too. This need for space varies by relationship and context. With close friends or family, the boundary shrinks. A hug, a whisper, a shared couch, these are welcome gestures. But with strangers or acquaintances, the buffer expands. In professional settings, maintaining a respectful distance signals courtesy and awareness. In crowded elevators or public transport, people often cope with the forced proximity by avoiding eye contact, turning away, or mentally zoning out. Cultural norms play a huge role. In Japan, personal space is meticulously observed, reflecting values of privacy and harmony. In contrast, Mediterranean and Latin American cultures may embrace closer distances as signs of warmth and connection. Misunderstandings can arise when these norms clash. What feels friendly to one person may feel invasive to another. Psychologically, invading personal space can activate the brain's threat response, increasing heart rate and stress levels. For individuals with anxiety or sensory sensitivities, this intrusion can be overwhelming. It's not just about physical proximity. It's about emotional safety. Respecting someone's space communicates empathy, trust, and social intelligence. In modern life, personal space extends beyond physical boundaries. loud phone conversations, shoulder surfing at ATMs, or standing too close in cues all breach this invisible zone. Even digital interactions like unsolicited messages or location tracking can feel like spatial violations. Respecting personal space is more than etiquette. It's a form of emotional literacy. It means reading body language, noticing when someone leans away, and adjusting accordingly. It means understanding that comfort zones differ and that honoring them fosters connection rather than distance. Ultimately, personal space is a quiet language we all speak. It's the pause before a handshake, the step back in a crowded room, the choice to sit one seat apart. And when we listen to it, when we respect its boundaries, we create a world that feels safer, kinder, and more attuned to the rhythms of human connection.

- Why you’re an emotional sponge and 5 Tips for Better Boundaries: Kind: captions Language: en - Hi I'm Dr. Tracey Marks and I make mental health education videos. Today's video is talking about how you can take on other people's emotions and how to stop doing it. And this is based on a viewer question from Kelly and she says, "Hi Dr. Marks. "I don't know why I always seem to attract "emotionally intense people. "I get overwhelmed by them and then I shut down "and don't have anything to do with anyone. "So now, I don't have anyone in my life. "I don't know why I end up with these people. "How do I stop doing this?" Thanks Kelly, for your question. I would start out by saying on the issue of attracting these people, I think you should see the attraction as a two-way mirror. There's something about you that makes the intensely emotional person want to be around you and handoff their emotions to you. But there's something about them that draws you to them and allow you to tolerate their behavior. Now what I'm going to say is not aligned with any specific theory of being an empath or highly sensitive person. Those are terms that have been researched and defined by others and have specific meanings to them. What I'm talking about is my opinion based on general psychodynamic principles. I think one of the reasons you absorb is having a low emotional intelligence. Emotional intelligence is the ability to recognize and manage your own emotions and recognize the emotions of others. With high emotional intelligence, you're able to distinguish between your emotions and someone else's. Making that distinction is really important if you don't wanna take on someone else's emotions and make them your own. To recognize your emotions, you must have a certain degree of self awareness. And it's one thing to recognize that you feel bad. But bad is vague and non-specific. You can feel ad because you're angry, insulted, bored, or feel empty. And all of those emotions are very different from one another but can be put under the umbrella of bad. So at any given time, if you're not clear on how something affects you, it's easy to take on the emotions of someone else and assume what they feel. Then, once you assume that other's person's emotions, you have trouble managing it on your end. Here's an example. Suppose you're taking a break at work and you're sitting at your desk watching a funny TikTok Video, your coworker, who you consider a friend, comes up to you and starts ranting about how she's so angry because her boss gave her a bad review and she didn't get her raise. So she doesn't see how anyone can work in this place with this kind of boss who doesn't care about anyone. And she doesn't know how long she's gonna stick around at this place. Two minutes ago, you were laughing at the dog video you were watching. But now you feel overwhelmed with anxiety about your position at work. Should you stay in this job? Is it even a a safe place to work? Later on that night, you have trouble sleeping because you may fear you may lose your job. Now no one told you that your job is in jeopardy. But you just have the sense that maybe it is. Back at work, your friend settles down and you even see her laughing with your boss about something. So your anxiety subsides and you start to feel that all is well. And things start to return to their usual. But you still lost a couple of weeks of poor sleep and feeling anxious. Now part two of this scenario is that this person is your friend and when she got this bad news, you're the only person she ranted to, probably because you're the only person who would listen. But if you're someone who doesn't always know what you feel, or doesn't feel strongly about things, at some level, you may be attracted to people who have strong, clear opinions. It's not a deliberate process of saying to yourself, I don't know how I feel so I feel whatever you do. Instead it's an unconscious process, meaning that it's beyond your awareness that helps you deal with your own emotional uncertainty by assigning someone else's emotions to yours. It's unsettling to feel vague about how something affects you. And it's reassuring to have someone who's more resolved about how they feel, swoop in and show you how to feel. And this is an automatic process that happens like a reflex. And what's the evidence for that? The fact that you keep attracting people with strong emotions that overwhelm you. You have to be drawn to them in some way. Another reason you may be drawn people with strong emotions that overwhelm you is being a people pleaser and needing to fix people. If you don't wanna disappoint someone, it's easy to take the handoff when they present their intense, negative emotion to you. Here's an example of this. Suppose your partner tells you that he had this horrible thing happen to him where he felt exploited in some way, like people were taking advantage of him. And then he explodes about it and then storms off. You're left standing there feeling upset about what happened to him and you feel bad for him. Nevertheless, you have plans with your girlfriends to go to the movies later that evening. So you start to get dressed and he asks, "Where the heck do you think you're going?" You remind him that you have plans with your girlfriends. He explodes at you about how could you even think about going out with your friends after what he's been through. If you cared anything about him, you wouldn't be able to go have fun. You think, gosh, I guess that is pretty insensitive of me to go our and have fun when he's so unhappy. So you cancel your plans. So to find something to do 'cause he stormed off in a room by himself, you binge watch something on Netflix and fall asleep on the couch. You see him the next morning and he says he didn't get a wink of sleep all night and he doesn't believe you really care about him because how could you even sleep so peacefully after something like that happened to him. The rest of the day, you feel awful and think, maybe you don't care for your partner as much as you should. You convince yourself that you're insensitive and you wish you could be a better person and you start thinking of ways that you could be a better partner to him. In this example, the people pleasing part of you puts up with his need to see you suffer so that he can feel validated. In fact, you don't see it that way. You really buy in to his assertion that the evidence that you care about him is that you take on the same emotion that he has and experience it for as long as he does. And as long as he's unhappy, you should be unhappy as well. But because you don't wanna disappoint him, you accept this assertion and own it. This scenario works a little differently for the person who wants to fix or help instead of please. So the same thing happens. He comes home and tell us about his upsetting experience that he had and you feel pain for him. He doesn't need to tell you not to go out with your friends because you're not in the mood anymore. You're so upset that you can't see yourself having fun with your girlfriends or if you do go, you spend the whole time with your girlfriends talking about his situation and how horrible those people were to him. Even if you don't take his side on the issue, you've taken on a project of healing him. And you're gonna heal him through your love. But while you're working to heal him, you're also experiencing the emotions as if it happened to you. Now these are not the only reasons that you can be an emotional sponge. We just looked at two reasons. But let's look at what you can do about this. The first thing is to increase your emotional intelligence. You start this by increasing your self awareness. In a previous video, I talked about getting into the habit of being specific about what kinds of emotions you're experiencing at any given time. I created these emotions cards to help expand your vocabulary when it comes to identifying general emotions and being able to make them more specific. You can download these cards from my website at MarksPsychiatry. The second thing you can do is stop and think about whether what you're feeling is coming from you or is it because the way someone else felt? Is the negative emotion you're experiencing something that was handed off to you by someone? Did you only start feeling this way because the person you care about felt that way. And keep in mind, it's not always bad to empathize with someone and react the same way they did. That leads to the next thing that you can do, which is learn to manage your emotions so that they don't overwhelm you. And there's many ways to do this. One way is to practice mindfulness meditation. I have a video on managing negative emotions that includes a mindfulness exercise that helps you observe anger in a neutral way and accept it and let it pass. So the idea is that you don't have to run away from every negative emotion that you see coming. But you wanna be able to build an emotional force field around you that allows you to be in the presence of negative emotions, observe them, interact with them but keep them from piercing your soul and taking over your thoughts and behavior. The fourth tip would be to ensure that you have proper boundaries between yourself and others. Sometimes this is as concrete as having a physical distance. So in this example that I had of the co-worker yelling at you about her experience, in that situation, it may be helpful to push back and let there be more physical distance between her and you. And it sounds very basic but it really can make a difference. Watching someone rant from a distance is an entirely different experience than having them rant right in your face. It's as if the further away they are, the more that you can keep what their experiencing separate from you. If they get too close into your personal space, you can lose sight of what's theirs and what's yours. The last tip has to do with the need to save people. And there's different reasons you may do this for some people, it helps give them a sense of control. You can take control of the negative things and make them positive. It could also be a way to distract from your own pain by focusing on someone else's pain. For some people, they need a damaged person to fix as a way of making themselves feel validated. So there's lots of motivations behind the need to save people and it can get complicated. But suffice it to say that if you notice this pattern with yourself, it's worth taking a step back and reflecting on what you're getting out of it. What's the payoff to you to change the way this person thinks or to make all negative things positive? And I'm not talking about trying to save your child from experiencing the pain of growing up. I'm talking about a pattern that shows itself, peer to peer with your adult relationships. Alright, that's it for now. I could go on, but I don't wanna overload you. But if you haven't gotten enough, check out this video on dealing with negative emotions. See ya next time.

- 5 ESSENTIAL TIPS to Master ZEN Interior Design at Home: Kind: captions Language: en here are five things you need to know to decorate in Zen interior design style your busy and stressful life might explain why many people love the Zen interior design style this style inspired by meditation practices aims to create a peaceful and Serene environment in your home in this video you will gain a deep understanding of Zen interior design we'll cover its defining characteristics the types of furniture you can use the color schemes suitable materials and Decor elements incl including types of rugs and Window Coverings finally we'll discuss the lighting needed to achieve the perfect look and feel of this interior design style you might wonder why Zen is included with other retro inspired Styles well Zen which means meditation in Japanese has roots and meditation practices from China in the 1600s it spread to Japan other parts of Asia Europe and the United States although Zen is not exactly an interior design style but a set of guiding principles on how to decorate your home a distinct look has emerged from these principles for Simplicity we'll refer to it as a style what is Zen interior design all about it's about creating a calm peaceful environment this is achieved through Simplicity decluttering is key removing unnecessary items to avoid distractions and Chaos in your mind let's dive deeper into its defining characteristics let's get started here are the five things you need to know about Zen interior design number one the furniture in a Zen home lowprofile furniture made of high quality wood with clean straight lines and no excess detail or ornamentation works best for example use a platform bed with white bedding in the bedroom complemented by a simple wooden nightstand and a few plants avoid overloading the room with Furniture keep it minimal in the living room opt for a Sleek simple sofa with a few cushions in neutral tones add a low coffee table made of natural wood use built-in storage solutions to keep the space clutter-free for the dining area choose a wooden dining table with matching chairs that have straight lines and no embellishments a bench can also be a good addition for a minimalist look number two the color scheme in Zen interior design the color schemes used are neutral white gray beige and brown to create a calming atmosphere a touch of black can be used sparingly green the color of Nature and blue the color of water are the common non-neutral colors used in a Zen home for example in the living room use white or beige walls as a backdrop add sense of green through plants or cushions a gray sofa can add depth without being overpowering for the bedroom soft white or light gray walls can be complemented with bedding in shades of beige or light brown a touch of blue in pillows or a throw can add a calming effect and for the bathroom use white or light gray tiles with wooden accents for warmth a few green plants can enhance the Tranquil feel number three the materials materials in a Zen style are simple and natural think wood bamboo and glass avoiding Plastics for Fabrics go with easy care natural materials like cotton and wool patterns are generally absent in Zen design if you can leave your windows be to let in natural light if you need window coverings choose bamboo blinds or simple light colored curtains Lightwood flooring is ideal but solid Stone floors like slate work too for rugs wool cotton or jute in solid neutral colors are great options for the furniture choose pieces made of high quality wood like Oak Walnut or bamboo avoid synthetic materials and opt for natural finishes for the flooring light wood like Maple or Birch is ideal for a more earthy feel consider Stone floors in areas like the kitchen or bathroom and for the textiles use natural Fabrics like cotton linen and wool for upholstery curtains and rugs keep patterns minimal and focus on texture number four the decorations artwork in a Zen home should be minimal choose one or two pieces per room that you truly love such as Japanese inspired art or black and white nature photos open shelving with a few accessories looks great only display items that you deeply love in a living room use a large piece of abstract art or a black and white photograph on one wall keep accessories minimal like a single sculpture or a simple vase for the bedroom a single piece of Japanese inspired art above the bed can serve as a focal point use open shelving sparingly displaying only a few cherished items and for the bathroom incorporate natural elements like stone or wood accessories a simple bamboo plant or an orchid can add A Touch of Elegance number five lighting maximize natural light but use soft low light from multiple sources when you need artificial lighting simple table lamps floor lamps and concealed lighting are perfect for example use lights hidden in bookcases or under cabinets avoid harsh lighting instead use hanging paper lanterns for diffused ambient light candles whether real or flameless add a cozy touch for the living room use recess lighting to create a soft ambient glow a floor lamp with a simple design can provide additional light for reading or relaxing for the bedroom incorporate soft warm lighting with bedside lamps or wall mounted sconces use candles for a calming atmosphere and for the bathroom use concealed lighting under the cabinets or mirrors a few well-placed candles can enhance the spa-like feel additional elements for Zen interior design style plants are a must in a Zen home good choices include airplants bamboo Money Trees bonsai trees and palm trees for flowers go with beautiful but simple orchids incorporate water features like a small fountain to enhance the Tranquil atmosphere for example in the living room a large bamboo plant or a fiddle leaf fig tree can serve as a natural focal point for the bedroom use smaller plants like airplants or succulents on nightstands or shelves and for the bathroom a small water feature or a few simple flowers can add a touch of Tranquility here's an example of a Zen bedroom imagine furniture with straight clean lines a lowprofile bed and lots of wood tones the color scheme includes white beige Brown and a touch of black lighting is a mix of table lamps floor lamps and recessed lighting artwork is minimal with just a couple of pieces and a bamboo plant adds a natural touch remember the key to Zen interior design is Simplicity and Tranquility by following these principles you can in your home stay tuned to our Channel because we will be designing a room using Zen style check the description below for products similar to those in this video that you can use in your own home

- Beyond Beige: Supporting Mental Health Through Design | Stephanie Vito, AIA | TEDxBuffalo: Kind: captions Language: en [applause] [music] A little over a decade ago, a doctor gave me a medical diagnosis that changed my life. The news was unexpected and completely blindsided me. For a few minutes, I even fell outside my own body. Maybe you can relate to the feeling. It was the hardest day of my life. But you know what I remember most vividly about that day? It wasn't the doctor or the way they delivered the news or even what I was wearing. No, it was the beige. Beige walls, beige flooring, beige furniture. Beige on beige with a highlight of more beige. The room was cold, dark, sterile, and offered no spatial comfort in that challenging moment. I mean, have you ever met anyone whose favorite color is beige? I just kept thinking, I don't want to be here. And it could have been so much better. There could have been views out to beautiful green space, more calming colors on the walls, access to fresh air, even. All of that would have helped, not with the difficulty of the news, but perhaps offered a bit more calmness in that challenging moment. Over a decade later now, I've forgiven Beige, but that moment has stayed with me. It's had a lasting impact. I'm now an architect who has dedicated her career to mental and behavioral healthcare architecture. I design recovery spaces for people who are struggling with severe mental health challenges, for people and their families who are often in crisis and sometimes having one of the hardest days of their lives. And I often think back to my own experience to help guide me. So I would like to share with you what I've come to believe. How space is designed has the power to be empathetic and kind, the power to comfort and heal or not. Whether we realize it or not, every aspect of our built environment has an impact on our mood and our emotions. Consciously and unconsciously, our mental health is constantly impacted by the physical spaces we live in. And while I've spent much of my professional career trying to make our healthcare spaces better, I believe it is time we rethink our communities and our cities through this lens. Mental health design belongs everywhere. We all have both physical and mental health. Yet, think of all the gyms, the parks, the playgrounds, the YMCA's that we infuse into our cities to help us actively invest in our physical health. Why couldn't we make the same investment investment in the built environment to help nourish our collective mental health? Think of all the busy airports, the shopping centers, the grocery stores. They're loud and socially dense and overwhelming. Now, imagine if these places had areas that people could step into to de-stress and self-regulate. What if we had mental health management spaces in every workplace to help us cope with the pressures of deadlines and other work-related stressors? And the schools where our children learn each day are those optimized for mental health in their current state. And our libraries and community centers, mental health design belongs everywhere. These spaces and resources would help everyone. And they'd also help those living with a diagnosible mental health illness. The data makes clear that one in four adults is currently living with a diagnosible mental health illness and around 20% of children are living with a probable mental health disorder. And if we expand the definition of mental health slightly, we can consider that around 70% of the adults in the United States have experienced at least one traumatic event in their lifetime which can significantly impact mental health. Mental health concerns are also elevated in communities for individuals from lower socioeconomic communities. Uncertainty around housing, income, safety, these concerns are elevated in these communities which can have an adverse effect on mental health. So when we think about mental health design everywhere, it's important that we prioritize communities where these strategies would have the greatest impact. It's also worth noting that mental health is a spectrum and we're all constantly moving along it at different stages of our lives. We all have to manage it. The ups, the downs, whether that's in an impatient setting, managing it at a workplace, at home, or in public. And with a significant portion of our population having a diagnosible mental health illness, we can't just reserve mental health design strategies for healthcare spaces. We must deploy them to rethink our communities and our cities. Over the last decade of designing mental health care facilities, more than 40 projects in 20 different states, I've identified some very important and broadly applicable design principles that truly make a difference in people's mental health. These design principles can help us think about how we uh can these design principles can help inform how we think about all different kinds of spaces, not just healthcare spaces. and they can help inform all of us on how to be more intentional about where and how we spend our time. These design principles are designing for the senses, designing to support psychological safety, and embedding freedom of choice. Let's walk through them, shall we? This is an image that I often put up during design presentations without any context. And I ask, "What kind of space do you think this is?" I sometimes get things like wellness suite or tech office, boutique hotel even. It almost always surprises people when I share that this is a core space at the University of Health at the University of Kansas Health System Strawberry Hill campus, an impatient mental health facility in downtown Kansas City. This is a space that was designed to stimulate the senses. Did you know that we actually have eight and not five like you might have been taught at school. The other three have some fairly complicated names, but are essentially all internal to us and have to do with movement, where your body is in space, and bodily function cues, like knowing when to go to the bathroom or the one we all try to avoid, feeling hangry. And this is important because it means we all have a different sensory profile and respond differently to inputs. This space is designed to stimulate the senses, sight, smell, sounds, touch, and other sensory inputs to help mental health patients on their recovery journey. This building also has remarkable access to daylight in nature, which is so important because as humans, we spend around 90% of our time inside. So, we must do more to bring the outside in and create new opportunities to do inside things outside through design. Views of the city skyline beyond and the Missouri River below are maximized for both patients and staff from multiple vantage points throughout the facility. There are calming colors on the walls, tactile materials throughout, large open spaces for fluid movement, choice of orientation within rooms, and of course, not so much beige. Let's talk about the second design principle to support mental health, designing for psychological safety. In our studio, we often say that a wall doesn't need to be just a flat surface. We can use it to create occupiable spaces along the periphery of a room. So someone with say social anxiety can still feel engaged in what's happening without being fully immersed in the center of the space. This is a strategy we've deployed at several children's hospitals throughout California. And you can see how this brings moments of safety and support and joy for these kids in these care spaces in new ways. We can bring great creativity into these spaces, allowing someone to sit fully supported with their back against the wall in a comforting niche or an al cove, a protected place that's appealing to any child really, but especially those that may have experienced any past trauma. Designing to support psychological safety also means providing clear wayfinding so all users, patients, staff, visitors can effectively find their way through space without any added stressors or anxiety. And the third principle, embedding freedom of choice. It's important in impatient settings to give people agency whenever possible so they don't feel confined or stuck. We can do this through big moves like space types and adjacencies or smaller moves like movable furniture and controllable lighting. At Prisma Health Behavioral Health Hospital, there are a variety of different kinds of activity and therapy spaces on unit that someone can choose. And we've located a fresh air porch directly across from the staff desk so all users, staff, patients, visitors, say caregiver or family member can step out and get some fresh air while they're here, listen to the sound of the rain or the birds without having to ask for permission. It's a simple yet impactful choice. At Shepard Pratt's Washington Baltimore campus, the building is purposely embedded in the landscape with porches, passive and active courtyards, landscaped walkways, and other opportunities that empower patients with choice on how they visually and physically engage with the surrounding landscape without sacrificing their desired privacy or safety. This level of choice is actually quite remarkable when you think of the design of mental health facilities from decades past. So, I've established the three design principles that truly make a difference in people's mental health. Let's use them to imagine how they can reshape our communities. - How Interior Design Affects Your Mental Health  #Neuroscience  #darkpsychology: Kind: captions Language: en Before you hear another word, take a look around the room you're in right now. You likely assume you chose how to feel when you sat down. You didn't. The color on the walls already made that choice for you. Color is not decoration. It is a direct line into your nervous system. The wavelengths in your environment actively shape your baseline stress levels, your ability to focus, and even how hungry you are right this second. Major retailers, architects, and brands have understood this for a long time. They have spent decades engineering physical spaces to trigger specific biological reactions in you. While our biological hardware is universal, cultural associations translate these signals differently depending on where you are. For instance, while western research contexts often link red to danger or urgency, those same wavelengths signal luck and prosperity in China. Decoding the science of color is about uncovering the invisible forces that dictate your daily behavior. To see how spaces control us, we have to look at what color actually is. An object reflects specific wavelengths of light. Your visual system catches them and your mind constructs the experience of color. As the light enters the eye, it doesn't all go to the image processing center. A portion of that signal branches off and routes straight down into a different area entirely. It hits the super kayazmatic nucleus or SCN, the brain's internal clock. From there, the SCN signals your body's master control room, regulating your hormones, your sleep cycles, and your emotional baseline. This physical pathway is why warm colors automatically hike up your heart rate and trigger an alert state, while cool colors slow your breathing and trigger a calm response. This hormonal cascade happens before you have consciously formed a single thought about the room. If a designer controls the color, they control your biology. Consider red. Exposure to high arousal red environments spikes your appetite, increases your heart rate, and induces a mild stress response. Fast food empires blanket their dining rooms in it because they need you slightly alert, eating quickly, and moving on. Blue operates on a different frequency. This wavelength lowers your heart rate, sustains your focus, and builds a physiological sense of safety. Tech platforms and financial institutions rely on blue because it makes you feel secure enough to hand over your private data and your money. Yellow offers energy, but it comes with a hidden biological cost. Its high luminance physically strains the eyes, causing rapid visual fatigue and irritability. This split layout compares how smart designers mitigate that strain. Apps use yellow in tiny isolated bursts, while massive retail warehouses use it to pull you in, then shift to neutral tones inside so you stay longer without getting overwhelmed. Every hue you encounter in a commercial space is a calculation designed to manipulate your brain state. We can zoom out even further. Evolutionarily, green meant water and safety. Because it sits at the center of the visible spectrum, your eyes require almost zero physical effort to process it. Brief exposure to green spaces causes a documented, measurable restoration of cognitive attention. When cities replace those restorative greens with gray monochrome infrastructure, a condition called chromatic deprivation sets in. This dominance of concrete geometry creates a specific physiological burden. Researchers link environments exactly like this to higher psychological stress and reduced cognitive well-being. Injecting saturation back into these concrete grids through murals and painted public spaces acts as a measurable public health intervention. At an urban scale, color stops being a tool for commercial manipulation and becomes foundational infrastructure for mental health. The speed of this biological response is staggering. Color dictates up to 90% of your initial impression of a product or a space. As this timeline shows, that judgment locks in at under 90 milliseconds. Your brain makes the call before your conscious mind even starts evaluating the details. This clarifies why the red buttons convert better myth persists. Your brain's attention system is simply wired to detect high contrast against a background. I tested this in my own office by replacing a high arousal red accent wall with soft blue glean tones. Within 2 weeks, my deep work sessions were noticeably longer. Understanding this microbiology gives you a practical tool to hack your environment for better personal performance. Reclaiming your environment starts with making specific functional adjustments. You can introduce blue or green to your workspace to lower cortisol and reserve warm high contrast colors strictly for moments when you need to draw focus. You also need to manage your circadian signals. Staring at blue light from screens after dark tricks your SCN into destroying melatonin, wrecking your sleep architecture. The painter was Kandinsky recognized this power decades ago. He wrote, "Color is a power which directly influences the soul." He knew it long before we had the fMRI machines to prove it. The colors on your walls and your screens are already doing their work. You only have one choice. Design your space with int.

- Designing an Autism-Friendly Home: Tips for Sensory-Friendly Spaces: Kind: captions Language: en Designing an Autism-Friendly Home: Tips for Sensory-Friendly Spaces Designing a home that is autism-friendly can greatly improve the quality of life for individuals with autism and their families. Creating sensory-friendly spaces can help reduce sensory overload, promote calmness, and enhance comfort. Here are some tips to consider when designing an autism-friendly home. 1. Sensory Considerations Pay attention to the sensory elements in your home. Minimize harsh or bright lighting by using dimmer switches or adjustable curtains. Use soft, muted colors on walls and avoid busy patterns that may be visually overwhelming. Opt for natural, non-toxic materials for furniture and flooring to reduce chemical sensitivities. 2. Noise Reduction Noise can be particularly overwhelming for individuals with autism. Consider installing soundproofing materials to reduce outside noise. Use noise-canceling headphones or white noise machines to create a more tranquil environment. Also, designate quiet zones within the home where individuals can retreat to when feeling overwhelmed. 3. Organization and Safety Create a well-organized and clutter-free environment to provide a sense of order and reduce anxiety. Ensure furniture and fixtures are safely secured to prevent accidents. Install safety gates and locks to create boundaries and promote a sense of security. 4. Sensory Integration Incorporate sensory play areas to help individuals with autism develop their sensory integration skills. Include items such as bean bags, sensory bins, swings, and textures for tactile stimulation. These areas can provide a safe space for individuals to explore and regulate their sensory needs. 5. Communication Support Incorporate visual supports such as schedules, visual timers, and communication boards to enhance communication and understanding. These tools can help individuals with autism navigate daily routines and manage their time effectively. Designing an autism-friendly home requires thoughtful consideration of sensory needs, organization, safety, and communication support. Implementing these tips can create a harmonious environment that supports the well-being of individuals with autism and their families. Want to learn more? Subscribe and Share!

  3. @kpaxian6044 [9 likes]: I am adult on the spectrum with severe sensory overwhelm issues. Mine center around light most of all. Bright lights and bright colours give me migraines and I even have a history of seizures. I have a range of lights in my apartment. I usually keep the lights low and have brighter lights at a greater distance from me. I do better with skylights and sunlight provided it is filtered softly. But not like...LEDs or halogens. 

So I think for many on the spectrum that I know...lights are a big one. Dimmable bulbs and having areas that are darker work best for many. I also do not like too many bright colours. I can deal better with bright colours in small doses when the colours are muted and only seem brighter if the overall area is darker. But if I get too much light especially from halogens...it makes me very woozy and dizzy and ups my anxiety.

I am also very sensitive to textures. That seems very stereotypical but it is true. I break out in hives with any wool, even cashmere so I own nothing made with wool or anything that is scratchy.

I do like really good scents. I will seek them out. But scents are personal. I am not big on florals or powdery scents. I like things like mild spicy scents or sandalwood. I actually keep a packet of pumpkin spice tea in a sealable bag that I can sniff when I am in a "scent free area" so it does not bug others. Or a halo peppermint rollerball to smell. 

Best of luck to building more sensory friendly places!

- Design Psychology: 8 Tips for a Cosy Home (Architect-Approved): Kind: captions Language: en In today's video, we're going to go over&nbsp; eight timeless, tried and true ways of making&nbsp;&nbsp; your home feel really cozy and really warm.&nbsp; We're going to skip the obvious stuff like: throwing a blanket on a sofa, lighting some candles, getting a pet... love me LOVE ME! no, today, instead, we're going to go through&nbsp; the psychology of some of these strategies,&nbsp; so it doesn't matter what budget&nbsp; and what style you prefer,&nbsp;&nbsp; you'll be able to furnish your&nbsp; home and make it feel really cozy. Yellow light in the 2,700 to 3,000 Kelvin&nbsp; range mimics a warm glow of a setting sun;&nbsp;&nbsp; this is why we spend thousands of&nbsp; dollars on flights to go to beaches,&nbsp;&nbsp; so we can watch the sunset 'cause everyone looks&nbsp; good and feels good in that yellowish glow.&nbsp; And if you can buy something that's going to&nbsp; let you have this effect every single day,&nbsp;&nbsp; why would you do anything different? So&nbsp;&nbsp; basically if you were to read all of these pages,&nbsp; what he's trying to say is that the combination&nbsp;&nbsp; of natural and artificial light bouncing off&nbsp; different surfaces is what creates a feeling&nbsp;&nbsp; of warmth in the space. The cumulative effect&nbsp; of light bouncing off every surface in the room&nbsp;&nbsp; needs to produce a warm yellowish glow, so the&nbsp; more warm-colour surfaces you have in your home,&nbsp;&nbsp; the more likely that the cumulative colour that is&nbsp; produced used in the space from the light is warm,&nbsp;&nbsp; and that slight tint will affect&nbsp; how everything in the room feels:&nbsp; people furniture&nbsp; food on the table.
