According to the concept of&nbsp;&nbsp; biophilia, humans have an innate connection&nbsp; to nature and natural material materials because when we surround ourselves with&nbsp; natural elements that reminds us of the forest,&nbsp;&nbsp; of meadows, of the mountains, we feel more&nbsp; at ease, more relaxed, and yes, more cozy.&nbsp; So to make it work in your&nbsp; home, opt for real wood;&nbsp; wooden accents, wooden furniture, wooden decor,&nbsp; wooden frames; you can't go wrong with wood.&nbsp; They just make your home feel timeless and warm. You can also incorporate materials like natural&nbsp;&nbsp; stone, marble terrazzo in your counter tops&nbsp; and your furniture, and your decor pieces,&nbsp; and even small pieces like cork coasters, woven&nbsp; basket or natural jute rugs can make a space feel&nbsp;&nbsp; just a little bit more cozy. Bring the outside in! Minimalist design has stripped&nbsp; many homes of a key element that&nbsp;&nbsp; makes the space cozy, and that is texture. Imagine a living room with a thick wool rug,&nbsp;&nbsp; wood paneling, velvet cushions&nbsp; combined with soft mood lighting.&nbsp; Doesn't this feel so warm and cozy and inviting? Now, in contrast, imagine the space&nbsp;&nbsp; with smooth white walls very sleek shiny floors,&nbsp; plastic furniture, polished surfaces, it may&nbsp;&nbsp; look clean but it feels cold, flat and depressing. Texture is really really really important because&nbsp;&nbsp; it's everywhere in nature, and we love nature.&nbsp; From the rough bark of a tree to the patterns&nbsp; on the leaf to the stones in the riverbed,&nbsp; texture all around us. It's what makes nature&nbsp;&nbsp; so rich and engaging. Well, that and fresh air. Our eyes crave engagement and texture&nbsp; offers that in a subtle, comforting way. When these textures are missing in our homes, it&nbsp; creates what some people call "visual silence" a flatness that's not just&nbsp; boring but tiring to the eye. Without this visual engagement from texture,&nbsp;&nbsp; your space feels really lifeless&nbsp; no matter how tidy it is. So to add texture back into your home&nbsp; to make it cozy, here's what you can do: mix materials like velvet, wool,&nbsp; linen rugs to add warmth and comfort.&nbsp; Skip bare walls, try textured wallpaper wall paneling wall moldings lime wash is also super&nbsp; popular for this exact reason,&nbsp;&nbsp; that it provides very nice calming&nbsp; colors but it also has texture. You can also choose textured art like&nbsp; woven tapestries, or incorporate objects&nbsp;&nbsp; around your home with unique surfaces like texture&nbsp; glassware, brush metal accents, some clay pots... everything that's not super sleek and shiny,&nbsp; but that has like a slight bumpiness to it. Take a look at these two images: which one do you find more cozy? Most of you would probably choose this one, but why? Uniform lighting makes&nbsp; everything flat and lifeless.&nbsp; Uneven lighting feels way more natural&nbsp; and inviting; they create an interplay&nbsp;&nbsp; of light and shadow which is very much&nbsp; like the light we find again in nature. the light in nature is dappled,&nbsp; constantly moving, constantly in flux.&nbsp; If you want to make your home&nbsp; feel really cozy and inviting,&nbsp;&nbsp; with all of these pools of light and&nbsp; dark shadows, here's what you can do: use lamps all around your home at night. Flickering fire light is&nbsp; one of the most comforting&nbsp;&nbsp; and cozy elements you can have in a home. There's something primal about fire that&nbsp; comforts us on a psychological level, unless you're the Hound from Game of Thrones. For centuries, fire has provided not just&nbsp; warmth but a sense of safety and relaxation,&nbsp;&nbsp; so to bring this feeling into your home&nbsp; if you don't have fireplace, you can: use candles group them together they make a great alternative. When you walk into a room, the way the furniture&nbsp;&nbsp; is arranged can make you feel either&nbsp; really comfortable, or a bit uneasy. This is because some rooms are far too open,&nbsp;&nbsp; you need to create enclosures&nbsp; in all these pockets of space.&nbsp; You have a couch sitting alone in the midle&nbsp; of the room, it's going to feel uncomfortable,&nbsp;&nbsp; but but if you were to add a lamp over it with&nbsp; a side table and maybe a plant and some flowers, it would immediately feel much better. This is enclosure in action. It's the same reason why when you're sitting&nbsp; under a tree, it feels more comfortable. The tree provides shelter and the shadow&nbsp; it casts provides a boundary which makes&nbsp;&nbsp; you feel a bit more sheltered&nbsp; and protected and enclosed. So this idea applies to every&nbsp; single space in your home as well. Spaces with defined boundaries,&nbsp; whether it's a canopy over a bed, a pendant light above a table, or&nbsp; furniture that's tucked into a corner,&nbsp;&nbsp; will help your brain feel more&nbsp; relaxed when you're in it. Think about the difference between walking through&nbsp;&nbsp; nature with the gentle slope of a hill and&nbsp; the flowing river winding all around you,&nbsp;&nbsp; versus walking through the sharp angular&nbsp; cityscape with pointy buildings and sharp edges. One feels really soothing and calm&nbsp; and the other one feels dynamic,&nbsp;&nbsp; and more alive but also more stressful. Curves work at relaxing us&nbsp; because our brains are wired&nbsp;&nbsp; to perceive these shapes as safe and comforting. On the other hand, sharp angular&nbsp; shapes often signal danger:&nbsp;&nbsp; jagged rocks Thorns Cactus knives. COSY or COZY?
Hi everyone! I've noticed lots of comments about whether it should be "cozy" or "cosy." Just to clarify, "cosy" is the British English spelling, and since I'm from the UK, it's the version I'm used to! Of course, both spellings are correct—it's just a matter of preference and where you're from. 😊

mean danger so we're wired to&nbsp; tune into unexpected noises this Instinct designed to Keep Us&nbsp; Alive now picks up on everything,&nbsp;&nbsp; from leaky taps to buzzing annoying electronics and while we no longer have&nbsp; to worry about being eaten,&nbsp;&nbsp; these random sounds still trigger stress responses so if you have sounds in your&nbsp; home that randomly emits noise fix them you can also think about how you can reduce&nbsp; disruptive noise by furnishing your home&nbsp;&nbsp; with a lot of soft surfaces that just absorbs the&nbsp; sound and stops them from bouncing around so much so everything from rugs curtains book on shelves they all absorb sound and reduce echoes during the day bright cool light&nbsp; energizes us and keeps us alert but in the evening we're meant to wind&nbsp; down, warm light mimics the Golden Tones&nbsp;&nbsp; of sunset or fire light signaling&nbsp; to the brain that it's time to relax the wrong lighting at night,&nbsp; when it's bright, cool,&nbsp;&nbsp; overly harsh can disrupt this resting process start by swapping out cold white&nbsp; light bulbs for warm toned ones these emit a soft amber glow&nbsp; that feels cozy and natural you should also layer your lighting so instead&nbsp; of relying on a single overhead fixture add table lamps floor lamps&nbsp; wall lights these create pools of light&nbsp; adding depth and softness to a space and here is a super important rule no odd colored lights please neon Blues greens or purples might look really fun&nbsp; but they do disrupt the natural harmony your&nbsp; brain craves stick to soft yellows or amber&nbsp;&nbsp; tones that creates a soothing environment that&nbsp; feels aligned with how we are wired to relax if this video was helpful be sure to check out my other videos which talks&nbsp; about how to create a cozy elegant timeless home&nbsp; and make sure to subscribe before you leave thank you byeeeee by the way, no cats were harmed or&nbsp; abandoned for the making of this video Coco was allowed to be back&nbsp; in after like 1 minute outside it's really nothing I love my cat so much byeeeeeee

These&nbsp; frequencies are organized into separate classes&nbsp;&nbsp; named after the letters of the Greek alphabet&nbsp; and they control various cognitive functions&nbsp;&nbsp; such as alertness, calmness and drowsiness. A concentrated source of plant fragrance is&nbsp;&nbsp; essential oils. These contain compounds known&nbsp; as terpenes which affect the brain when inhaled.&nbsp; For example, the fragrance of mint increases&nbsp; the activity of the alpha class of brain waves,&nbsp;&nbsp; boosting our alertness while lavender, on the&nbsp; other hand, increases the activity of theta&nbsp;&nbsp; brain waves making you calmer and drowsier. So the scientists prepared four samples;&nbsp;&nbsp; whole Wishing Roses and Wishing Rose&nbsp; petals which could both be smelt and seen;&nbsp;&nbsp; a blind which is a rose but covered so&nbsp; that it can only be smelt and not seen;&nbsp;&nbsp; and, finally, they prepared a control which could&nbsp; be smelt but not seen, in this case plain water.&nbsp; For the blind sample, an increase in&nbsp; parasympathetic nerve activity was&nbsp;&nbsp; detected indicating the rose's&nbsp; sedative effect as expected.&nbsp;&nbsp; However, for the whole flowers and the petals,&nbsp; the sedative effect strangely seemed to decrease.&nbsp; The scientists then recalled another&nbsp; pink flower known as the Petunia hybrida&nbsp;&nbsp; whose colour was found to uplift subjects&nbsp; and make them feel more energized.&nbsp; Based on this, the scientists concluded&nbsp; that it was the energizing pink colour&nbsp;&nbsp; of the Wishing Rose that neutralized&nbsp; the calming effect of its fragrance.&nbsp; Isn't it interesting that according to the&nbsp; psychological Palms test, a rose's scent and&nbsp;&nbsp; appearance calms people down but according to the&nbsp; physiological parasympathetic nerve activity, the&nbsp;&nbsp; rose's scent and appearance have two completely&nbsp; different yet both still equally positive effects?&nbsp; What more is there waiting to be discovered in&nbsp; the plant world of colours and smells? - A Guided Meditation on the Body, Space, and Awareness with Yongey Mingyur Rinpoche: Kind: captions Language: en ﻿A Guided Meditation on the Body, Space & Awareness Yongey Mingyur Rinpoche Welcome! I’m very happy to share with you some meditation instructions. It is very simple: Please keep your spine straight. Just straight, but don't worry too much about perfection. I have to stay there, perfectly! Just keep straight. And please close your eyes. Just close your eyes like you’re sleeping. You don’t have to close them too tightly, or in any special way. And relax your muscles in your body. Relax the muscles in your head, forehead, face, ears, cheeks, throat, upper body, arms and hands, lower body, legs and feet. Just totally relax, yet with your spine straight. And bend your neck forward a little bit. It's almost like the weight of your head rests on your neck. You can effortlessly rest. Each part of the body rests on the one below. And in this way, you feel like you’re resting but there’s also some strength, so you can find balance. All the parts of your body are resting, one on top of the other. But, at the same time, there’s a strength within you. Your spine is straight. Sometimes you feel a little bit of gravity. And sometimes you feel some sensations. Just let it be. Whatever feeling is in your body, just be aware and let it be. When I was young, my father used to tell me, "Let your body be on the cushion, and let your mind be in the body." This way, you join body and mind together. Your mind comes into the body. Just be aware of your body, and be aware of any sensation or feelings in your body. Pleasant feelings, joy, bliss, or unpleasant feelings, tightness, pain, or neutral feelings, maybe tingling, or no feeling. So, with any type of feeling, just be aware and let it be. OK, for a few seconds, and relaxed. OK. Now, you can expand your awareness beyond your body, to what surrounds you. Maybe there are some sounds, or some smells, or some sensations, like your room temperature is a little bit too hot or cold, or some noise, like people chatting, or a car, a train, or airplanes moving around, or your neighbors making noise. Anything. Just be aware. Just know. Just recognize there are sounds. And your awareness becomes more open, and vaster. Stay there and be aware of any phenomena for a few seconds. Now, you can expand your awareness beyond your immediate surrounding. Feel the space around you, above you, and be aware of that space. Further, deeper. All the phenomena are occurring in the space, changing in the space, dissolving back into the space. Like clouds, they come into the space, remain in the space, and dissolve into the space. And, now, you can expand your awareness even more. Go beyond the clouds, into space, which is boundless. Space, which is open in all directions, everywhere. Be there. Don’t worry, you’re not going to get lost. Stay there for few seconds. OK, now, please slowly open your eyes, and be present here. And rest your mind, also — not only the body. How do you rest your mind? For example: If you like to do physical exercise, like jogging in the garden or park, or hiking in the hills or mountains, maybe for a few minutes or hours... let’s say one hour... After you finish your jog, you may use a nice seat or a rock, or a grass field. Sit on the grass field and, with a big sigh, “Ahh,” just rest. You don’t have to meditate, just let your mind rest as it is and the body, also. Just rest... Maybe you feel like, “Oh, there are so many thoughts, so many emotions occurring in my mind. What should I do with those emotions, those thoughts?” Same. Same as before, you just be aware. If there are thoughts and emotions, you’re just aware of thoughts and emotions, what I call “blah blah blah,” just look at the “blah blah blah,” be aware, and let it be. Same as when you’re aware of your body, aware of sounds and sensations, now it’s the same: Just be aware of thoughts and emotions. Let them come, and let them go. But, if you cannot be aware of them, it’s also fine. You're in a non-conceptual state of mind, but not too long — only for a few seconds. Again, when you see thoughts and emotions, or forms, sounds, smells — anything, just be aware... for a few seconds. If anything occurs in your mind, in your body, in your perceptions, just be aware, and let it be. OK. Now, you can apply this awareness, this openness, this awareness with any situation in your life, with any activities in your daily life. Short time, many times, everywhere, anytime. Seven days a week, 365 a year. In this way, you can fill your mind with joyful, peaceful experiences, and you will develop your inner love, compassion, and wisdom. Thank you!

  6. @becca1189: Excellent point on individual needs!!! 

As a night-owl, I've read about studies that show natural night-owls & people who work night shifts should minimize daylight exposure. Not eliminate, but minimize. Exposure to daylight is still positive & encouraged. However, too much exposure near bedtime or waking in particular can disrupt sleep patterns (some night-owls, like me, have difficulty falling asleep when it's light out while others don't get sleepy until the sun starts to come up -- waiting until the sun is fully up isn't good though). I turn in shortly before dawn, which has helped me a lot. Trying to get errands completed right before bed time is too stressful, so getting up in the early afternoon to get things done before work is ideal for me. 

Another thing that I've found helpful in terms of sleeping well: changing my waking schedule instead of my sleep schedule when adjusting to/from daylight savings time. Pick daylight/standard, whichever works best for you, & stick to it. Clocks are tools that help us plan our day, we don't have to "obey" them. Took me far too long to figure that out. 

Also, as an ADHD person, I get stressed if things are "too" tidy. ❤🧡💛💚💙💜

  1. @Caroline_Winkler [2400 likes]: Wow! The original tone of this comment was celebratory, but I'm a bit surprised after reading some of the comments, so I'm updating this with some notes. 

1. I hear that you prefer my old mockups. That's great feedback, and works fine for me. In this video I created my usual photoshopped room mockups, and then had AI convert them to 3D renderings, because I assumed that would be more helpful for viewers to see the vision. I see that that was an incorrect assumption. I have registered your feedback.

2. I made this video for the college students who watch my videos. I received multiple requests from them for a dorm room video, and I seek to serve. This video involved a little more than 40 hours of work to create, including several days researching the current dorm room standards from current college students sharing their rooms online - to ensure I was up to date on dorm room allowances and standards, and more importantly to hear from college students themselves what they like/disliked in their dorm room setups. So my layout advice here does not come from my personal opinions about what *would be fun*, but from current college students themselves who shared videos on tiktok and instagram reflecting on the setups and layouts they most liked with their roommates and sharing tips for upcoming students. I do research like this for all of my videos in order to best represent the people the video is for. I am surprised and then to see so much commentary assuming that college students are not able to compromise or collaborate with their roommates - assuming that the greatest value of a freshman student is to have their own window, rather than to make friends and find a place where they feel like they belong. I have to admit that these unbased criticisms feel frustrating to me not simply because of the amount of from-the-mouths-of-students research that went into this video, but more largely because of a very negative assumption that people are only selfish and self serving and cannot be trusted to compromise. I might believe that narrative myself if I had not collected feedback from current students themselves, not to mention compromised in this very way with my own freshman roommate (she got the window, we thought nothing of it) and seen the same among many other roommates. There are greater pursuits than who-gets-what.

3. There does seem to be a lot of variety both in dorm rooms themselves, and dorm rules. I do address this and I hope it is understood that I do my best to cover what I can in one short video. If some aspects do not apply to you, or to the room you had 20 years ago, please feel free to ignore that part. 

I put a lot of care into this video, as I do with all my videos. I hope all that work still has some value. 
Caroline

something that might be interesting to you please subscribe every week we'll work together working through the essentials and the basics of gardening so by the time the gardening season is in full swing you guys will be ready to go now let's get into the gardening basics for beginners starting with eight easy steps okay now let's start with step one which is know your zone and this is really important if you watch videos or read books that are written by gardeners that live in different climates than you and thus different zones than you the map is divided up into usda hardiness zones which defines a certain range of climate conditions this will tell you how hot and how cold your area is and thus what plants will survive and thrive in your garden to find your zone is very easy just type into google something like growing zone and then your zip code and your zone should pop up right away once you know your zone then you'll know what kind of plants are available to you for instance i live in zone 9b if i was to look up an arctic fire dogwood from proven winners i would see that this shrub is hardy in zones two through seven since this plant is hearty in zones lower than mine i can safely assume that our summer heat will kill this plant alternatively if you live in a zone that is lower than the plant's hardiness zone such as a petunia that is only hardy in zones 10 through 11 which is higher than my zone 9b i can safely assume that the cold in our winter will kill this plant this is usually what we call annuals in that the plant will survive for one of our growing seasons until the cold kills them in the winter and we have to plant new ones next year so speaking of annuals i think it is important for new gardeners to understand the different types of plants annuals perennials shrubs and trees if you go to your local nursery and see all these beautiful and colorful plants you need to know if they're going to last beyond one season or come back next year and next year and next when i started gardening i wanted the biggest bang for my buck so i always focused on perennials that i knew were going to come back the next year the next year and the next now the trade-off with that is that usually perennials have less color than annuals perennials bloom for less of the season than annuals so it's a trade-off so to break it down annuals will live for only one season or one growing year however annuals tend to be more colorful and have a longer blooming season if you watched my first youtube video on supertunia vista bubblegum you will have seen that these flowers bloom a bright vibrant pink from march when i planted them all the way through november good examples of annuals in most gardening zones are petunias and super bells in my zone zone 9b a common perennial is lantana now in most zones in the country this is an annual and most people treat this as an annual it's a beautiful sun-loving heat-loving plant that we are lucky enough to call a perennial here in northern california so speaking of perennials perennials will regrow every spring however they generally have a shorter blooming period during the year common perennials in my area are daylilies hostas hellebores most salvias just to name a few shrubs are similar to perennials in that they'll become permanent elements in your garden shrubs can either be deciduous which means they will lose their leaves in the winter or evergreen which means they will keep their leaves green throughout the winter and provide great winter interest shrubs are usually woody stemmed and obviously smaller than a tree finally the last type of plant in your garden will be a tree the trees are pretty self-explanatory we know what trees look like however we have to remember that trees start small they start as babies so what might look like a small shrub could actually grow over the years into a large specimen tree that we're all looking for so step three in my basics for beginner gardeners is consider your watering system new gardeners need to think about how they're going to water their garden are they going to hand water with a hose and a watering can or set up an irrigation system and have drip put to most plants i live in a pretty hot and dry climate so if i relied on hand watering i'd be out here for way too long every day so the time spent installing a drip system in my garden was well worth it however if you lived in a cooler zone that got more water during the summer and during the winter hand watering might be great for you i think the best thing to do for new gardeners is take a walk around your neighborhood look down and see if you see an irrigation or a drip system if you do most likely that's the way to go in your area and you might want to look into installing one yourself or consider hiring someone to install one for you so this brings me to gardening step four which is actually don't over water your plants this tip is for indoor gardeners as well as outdoor gardeners when i first started gardening i thought the more water the better that's what the plants need right and i remember giving this beautiful bougainvillea a gallon of water every single day even as it was losing its blooms and losing its leaves and i just didn't know that i was over watering this drought resistant dry loving plant i was basically killing this poor plant with kindness most new gardeners over water their plants just have that in the back your mind when you are considering how much to water your plants they usually need less water than you think okay so step five in my gardening basics for beginners is to watch out for pests most gardens will deal with their fair share of pests and that can ruin all your hard work very quickly you will know you have pests by looking closely at your flowers or leaves if they look chewed on you most likely have something in the garden taking a little snack as a beginner the easiest thing to do is to take a sample of a leaf to your local garden center to have them diagnose it for you they should be able to tell you what could be eating your plants and what you should do about it here in northern california the main pests i deal with are bud worms that will eat your entire flower off of your plant if they get to it earwigs snails slugs white flies just to name a few there are easy ways to take care of this problem and manage these pests you just have to know what you're dealing with to know what to do step six your plants are hungry just like humans we need water and food to survive your plants need water and food to survive as well each type of plant has a different fertilizing requirement and a good rule of thumb to think about is the more blooms the more color the plant has probably the more fertilizer it's going to need take for example my supertunia vista bubble gums they're probably the most abundantly colorful plant there is and therefore they need food every week during the growing season compare this to a perennial that only blooms for a few months out of the season and they might only need a dose of fertilizer once or twice a year i think that once i started actually feeding my plants they became more vibrant and put on a show to thank me i like to keep my fertilizing schedule set in the calendar on my phone so i have reminders to tell me when it's time to go feed my plants step seven is keep a plant journal this is a ton of information and there's no way anyone can remember all of it i like to keep my plant journal in my phone so i can look back on all the information that i have i like to look on the plant tag and note when i can expect it to bloom how much and when i should be fertilizing and also when and how i should prune the plant if needed having this allows me to look back on my notes from when i first bought the plant so i can remind myself what i need to do to treat the plant accordingly so this has been a lot of information i know i felt And so a huge mistake you can make, as a beginner gardener, is planting things that don't match to the sun and light availability of an area. The best way to do that is observation. Maybe 8:00 AM, 12:00 PM, 4:00 PM - go out and just kind of see where the shadows fall. And just remember that this is actually going to change based on the time of year. So a really good resource I'll leave down in the description. It'll allow you to drag over the months and it'll show you how the sun will fall over your property. You can type in your exact address. But again, remember planting is the most important time. If you make a mistake in planting, you've locked that plant in stone. Unless you're growing in a container, which is another great way to mitigate some of these mistakes. But please remember, do not plant in an area that does not have enough sun for the plant you're trying to grow. Mistake number three is planting too far from a water source. So I want to show you what I've had to do. I don't have irrigation set up here at the Epic Garden. I actually have ONE outlet here for water on the side of my house that's really inconveniently placed. It's in the middle of my house between the front and the back yard. And so I've used a hose splitter here and then I've split that many times over. Of course, I'm going to get irrigation. But the reason why you don't want to do this is because again, it's one of those laziness things. If you're going to have to lug water over, you might just decide not to do it that day. And then the day gets too hot. The plants die and you're in a big world of hurt. And you've again, killed some plants. You don't want to do that. So let me show you what I've done. Again, this is not the best solution. I'm still getting irrigation set up here, but hey, if it works, it works. If it lets me access water then I'm happy. Like I mentioned, the first thing I've done is split this. So we go to the front yard with one of the hoses. Now what you could do here is you could just use a standard hose. But I've put this into a Hoselink, which is a hose bib that has a retractable reel right here. So this one can extend out about 82 feet and it can go just about anywhere in this front yard garden. So I've got perfect access in the front yard. The second hose comes through right here. We're going to wind around the back. Again, if it works, it works. It's not the most clean setup, but we've gone to another hose bib right here with the Hoselink. And I can access everything in this little temporary orchard as well as all of the back garden back there. Mistake number four, one I admit I have made quite a bit, especially in my first few years of gardening is not respecting the power of the mulch. We must respect the power of the mulch. This is just some bare potting mix right here, recently amended. And sure, yeah, I could plant right into this and I'm sure I would be okay. But I can reduce my watering needs, protect the soil, protect any sort of splash-up disease type of issues with the use of some kind of mulch. Now, if you've been watching this channel a while, you know I'm obsessed with this shredded straw mulch from a company called GardenStraw. It's not available here in the U.S. I should have it available on my store sometime around April 2021, but don't let the fact that you can't get the exact straw that I use prevent you from using mulch. There's plenty of different things that you can use. You can of course, source straw in your own area. You can use grass clippings. You can use shredded leaves, fall leaves if you're in an area that gets a lot of leaf drop. I'm jealous, I don't. And fall leaves are a fantastic cover. Now the couple of things that you want to keep in mind with mulch. You want to make sure that you lay down a thick enough layer. I would say at least two inches or so if possible. And just because I'm in a raised bed right here doesn't mean that you should only mulch a raised bed. You should mulch your inground plantings and you should also mulch even smaller containers like some grow bags or just some patio style containers with something like a straw. You can do it. It is so, so beneficial. It's going to reduce the amount that you need to water. It's going to protect the surface of the soil and there's just a million and one different ways that you can do it. I've got a whole video on cheap sources of mulch that you should check out. But remember to mulch your garden. Mistake number five is not preparing the soil, not taking the time to prepare the soil before you plant. Now, if you're growing in a raised bed or a container that just means selecting the right container mix, which I'll do a video on this spring. But let's just take this bed for example. This is the no dig bed that I did in collaboration with Charles Dowding. And many of you who watched that video, you'll know that even during the making of that video I was critiquing my own process as compared to Charles, who is the master of the no dig garden bed. And so I actually ended up making some changes to this bed based on some of the mistakes I thought I made and then later knew I made in the soil preparation. So what I did here is I added a bunch of pumice to this to lighten it up. I was noticing that I was getting a little stunted growth here in the no dig bed because I think the compost mixture was just holding way too much water. Just way too much. And so that was a soil preparation mistake that if I didn't know that, and I had a couple of years of gardening under my belt, I would've just let that be. And then these cabbage plants would have just sat here doing nothing for three, four months because they have no ability to actually take in nutrients and grow. So soil preparation. Now, if you're growing inground, the first thing I recommend wholeheartedly, it's a little annoying to do, is to get a soil test. You have to do this to know what type of soil you're working with. I have a video on some DIY soil tests you can do, but nothing's going to beat hitting up your local nursery and saying, hey, can you put me in touch with someone who can do a soil test? And they'll give you not only the composition of your soil, but the nutritive qualities of your soil, how much organic matter is in the soil. Are you deficient in any particular macro-nutrient as well as, if you want, it's probably a little bit of extra money, you can get a heavy metal and salt report as well. Just to see if there's anything really funky going on with your soil, especially when you're planting inground in a new space because you just don't know what happened in the decades before you lived in this place. So soil preparation is a massive, massive mistake that you can avoid, of course, by doing the preparation. Mistakes number six and seven, I'm going to give you a two-parter here in this exact bed, is planting things that you don't like to eat. So here I have some beets and some kale. Fortunately, I have learned to like beets a lot more. I like to roast them up now, but kale I've always liked. So these are two things that I enjoy eating. Now I think what a lot of beginners will do is they might Google easiest plants to grow, beginner gardening suggestions, things like that. And then they'll just grow whatever that says without any regard for if you like to eat that plant. Now, look, I love growing weird plants, unique plants, plants that maybe I don't even like to eat. So I do break this rule for the love of the grow, for the love of the garden. I want to see something interesting and maybe I'll just give it to someone else who does like it. But if you're trying to grow a garden for your family and maybe some friends and of course yourself, pick the things that you actually like to eat. It's a little silly to grow something, spend all this time and care, to never harvest it. And even if you do harvest it, not use it, it's just a little bit of a waste. And number seven, the two-fer here is this is a pretty densely packed bed now that it's grown in. But I will confess that when things were getting a little busy here at Epic Gardening, I was not spending as much time in this particular bed as I would have wanted to. And so I have the beets that I direct sowed as well as the kale that I direct sowed. But I usually put a couple of different plants in per hole, a couple of different seeds, just to guarantee that something germinates. Now with beets you don't need to do that. Beets actually have a compound seed of sorts. And one what looks like a seed actually has multiple seeds within it. But still you have to thin that out. So you can plant, number one you can plant too densely seed to seed. So you can actually just plant them too close together. But number two, you can not thin them out after they've germinated. So what happened in this particular bed, and of course now it looks really good and I'm really excited, no disease, beautiful structure on the beets and the kale. But what was happening before was I had two, three plants right here, two, three plants right here, two, three, right there. The beets had a couple of different beets all trying to grow next to each other. And you can do it, they're just going to be a lot smaller. And that's what happened. They were just a little bit small, still healthy, but small. And as soon as I thinned them out and gave them some room to breathe, they all blew up in size. So while small space gardening is where I came from, and I love cramming plants in small spaces, you still have to respect there is a certain limit upon which you can cram things in. Mistake number eight is planting things at the wrong time of year. And I think this hits warmer climate gardeners. More than colder climate gardeners. Because colder climate gardeners, you have a. True beginning and end to your season with your first and last frost dates. And you can plant things a little early, a little late, sure. But you're not going to walk outside in the middle of the snow and be like, oh, it's a great time to grow tomatoes. It's just not going to happen. Whereas in the higher zones - zones, nine, 10, 11- you can get tricked because it's really not too cold at any point in time. I would say maybe about 40 degrees Fahrenheit at the absolute lowest in some of these zones. And so it looks like a time that you could plant just about anything. And so I'll get questions from people saying, oh, I planted my tomatoes. I planted my, you know, my cucumbers and it's November. Are they going to do okay? And the answer is technically yes, they're not going to just immediately die because they know it's the wrong time to be planted, but they're not going to thrive. And so you have to respect the time of year that a plant wants to grow. A summer crop like a tomato or a cucumber of course is going to be best done in late spring through summer. Maybe you can get a second planting of that if you're in a warmer zone. But you really do have to respect this. And for me, in my warmer zone, I need to respect that there's certain plants that like it colder that I just can't grow in the summer. I'm going to struggle to grow cilantro for example. I'm going to struggle to grow leaf lettuce in full sun in the summer, of course. So right now in spring it's the perfect time for me to grow some of those crops and either preserve them, although maybe not for the lettuce and the cilantro, but just respect the season and you'll have a lot more success. And if you want to know how to do that, what I suggest is watching my video on hardiness zones explained. That will kind of give you the primer on that. And then honestly, ask experienced gardeners, which leads us to our final tip. Our final mistake is to not lean on the experience of others. Gardening is a craft has been passed down generation after generation after generation, all cultures around the world. And there guaranteed are people alive right now, probably within a mile of you, that know a lot more than you may ever know about gardening. Because they've been doing it since they were 15 and they're 80 now. And so ask them how they do things and lean on them. There's a couple of different ways you can do this. Number one, if you have someone in your neighborhood that you know gardens, knock on their door. I guarantee you a gardener is going to want to talk about gardening with you until they're blue in the face. Number two, you can go to a local county extension office. Just Google your city and then extension office and it should show up. You can call them, you can email them. They're literally volunteers, master gardeners that their whole job is to help you succeed in your climate and in your area. And then third, engage with the gardening community. I actually created a Discord server for Epic Gardening. We have about 3000 people in there right now sharing based on where they are in the world. Based on the type of crop that they're growing. And it's basically just like an online chat room. So check it out. I created it. We're having a lot of fun in there right now! So I highly recommend you check that out. But until next time, hopefully these tips helped you avoid some mistakes in your upcoming season of gardening. Good luck in the garden. And keep on growing.

- 8 Design Tips for a Cosy Living Room (Architect Explains): Kind: captions Language: en In today's video, we're going to go over eight timeless tried and true ways to make your living room look cozy and feel so comfortable. Low ceilings, or the illusion of them at least, make a room feel a lot more intimate and warmer and cozier. Because when you bring the ceiling down, it makes you feel more cocooned and sheltered and like protected. In contrast, high ceilings point towards the sky. They feel more awe inspiring but emotionally distant. Just think about how you feel when you're in a really grand library or a cathedral. And these grand spaces with incredibly high ceilings make you feel very small and a little bit insignificant, which is cool in certain spaces, but not if you want a cozy living room where you just want to curl up with a book and your cat. So, if you're one of these very fortunate people watching my channel who have a massive house and incredibly high ceilings, but you want certain rooms to feel cozier, like a family living room, here's what you can do. Avoid placing seating right in the path of movement or in really hight traffic areas because then people just feel really unsettled. I think this is why, for example, when you go to a restaurant, you never want to pick the seat that's right next to the toilet or next to the kitchen door. If we were to compare these two living spaces and pick a cozy one, I think it's pretty clear that the room that looks a bit warmer is also a little bit more cozy and inviting. I mean, I think it's really easy to think that warm colors means just painting your walls yellow, red, terracotta, and calling it a day. But it's not quite as simple as that. So the real source of the warmth is not the color of the room, but it's the color of the light that fills the room. We find rooms with warm colored light more cozy because light with red and yellow tones calms our nervous system. It makes people's skin look healthier, food more appetizing, and everything just looks more inviting. Cold colored light does the opposite. So to make your living room feel cozy during the day and night, make sure the overall color of the light feels warm. Prioritize yellows, reds, oranges, and warm wood in dark or north facing rooms. - How Minimalism Can Improve Your Mental Health: Kind: captions Language: en Mental health is an important topic of conversation these days and rightfully so. Mental health diagnoses in the US increased by nearly 40% between 2019 and 2023. Now, it's probably true that more attention to the topic in years past would have helped many, but we can't change the past. We can only choose how to move forward. And so I'm glad to see that mental health is becoming a greater priority in our lives and in our conversations because it matters. It affects how we think, how we feel, how we relate to others, and how we live our lives. And for many of us, life can often feel like we're just trying to keep our heads above water, managing stress, overwhelm, discouragement, or burnout. I've mentioned in previous videos about the mental health benefits of minimalism, and I'm certainly not the only one highlighting the connection. Researchers and psychologists are increasingly pointing to the correlation between clutter and anxiety, between too many choices and decision fatigue, between too much stuff and stress. In fact, I'll put a few links in the description below for you. There are studies showing that clutter increases our stress levels, that clutter limits our ability to focus, that clutter affects our sleep, that clutter lowers our mood and raises our cortisol. Clutter can even lead to unhealthy eating, poor habits, and increased conflict at home. The research is clear. Too many physical possessions in our homes and lives negatively impacts our mental and emotional well-being. But what's often missing from this conversation is why? Why does owning fewer possessions lead to a clearer and more healthy mind? How exactly does minimalism improve our mental health? So, I'd like to explain why and do it in a way especially for anyone who might be skeptical about the connection. Minimalism will improve your mental health. And here are seven reasons why. Number one, minimalism helps us feel in control again. Minimalism allows us to take back control. And I don't just mean that we take control of our physical surroundings by removing the clutter. But minimalism forces us to live a more intentional life. It helps us live a life that's not just drifting with the masses, society, or influenced by other people. Minimalism requires us to take control of our resources, our passions, our values, our attitudes, our worldview, and ultimately our choices. When we start living life intentionally focused on what's most important to us, we find more pride and joy in it. Number two, minimalism helps us focus on what matters most. When we stop chasing physical possessions, we start asking better questions, starting with, "What do I really value? What do I want my life to be about? What is important to me? Minimalism forces questions of values upon us. And those questions help us get more clear on what matters and what doesn't, how we want to live our life and how we don't. But even more than that, minimalism removes the distractions so we can begin actually pursuing those values that we hold most dear. And when our lives align with our values, when we start living for something bigger than ourselves, our view of ourselves begins to change as well. We begin to see that we're both capable and actively living lives that matter. Number three, minimalism creates space both around us and within us. Minimalism clears clutter and creates space in our homes and in our minds. And both are important. Every physical possession carries weight and takes up space both physically and mentally. We can see the physical. Every object on a shelf or box in the garage makes our living space smaller. The mental space our possessions require is harder to see and measure, which makes it, I think, even more dangerous. Most of us have no idea how much of a mental burden our possessions have become until we begin to remove them. But every pile, every mess, every undone task is a subtle reminder of what still needs to be handled. And it serves as a visual reminder of money and time wasted. So when we clear the clutter, something shifts. The house feels lighter. Our minds feel lighter. And so do we. The fourth reason that minimalism leads to better mental health is that minimalism results in generosity. One of the most life-changing shifts that happens when we start owning less is that we find the margin to give more. We stop thinking about what we're missing and we start noticing all that we have. And when we see our excess more clearly, we naturally want to share it with others, with people we love, with those in need, and helping causes that And here's what's so beautiful about generosity. It's not just good for others, it's good for us. Studies consistently show that generous people experience lower levels of stress, stronger relationships, and more overall life satisfaction. And minimalism opens the door to that kind of life, which is another reason it improves our mental health. Number five, minimalism helps us focus on people. When we're constantly managing stuff, it's hard to make room for connection. Our time is spent cleaning, organizing, shopping, and maintaining. In fact, some studies estimate that we spend two hours each day managing our possessions. So, when we remove the excess, we make more space for people. And relationships are one of the greatest protectors of mental health. In fact, according to the longestr running study on human happiness, 85 years in counting, the most consistent finding they've learned is that positive relationships keep us happier, healthier, and help us live longer. We were created for community, and minimalism helps us find it. Number six, minimalism reduces fatigue. We don't always notice how much energy our possessions demand from us until they're gone. But every item in our home requires attention. It must be cleaned, organized, repaired, stored, moved, and eventually discarded. And when we're surrounded by too much, we start to feel it in our minds, in our bodies, even in our shoulders. But minimalism doesn't just alleviate physical fatigue. It alleviates decision fatigue as well. A simple home leads to a simpler day with fewer unimportant decisions to make, which means we're able to make better decisions on things that matter and better decisions make for better living. And number seven, minimalism quiets comparison. One of the most damaging habits to our mental health is constantly comparing our lives with others. Social media, advertising, even the car in our neighbors driveway can subtly whisper that we're not measuring up. But minimalism invites a different message into our lives that we already have enough. When we stop chasing what others have, we begin to see the beauty of our lives more clearly. Gratitude replaces envy. Peace replaces anxiety. Calm replaces turmoil. And we begin to feel comfortable in our own skin. Again, the mental health benefits of minimalism are real and they're available to all of us. The most beautiful thing about this list of mental health benefits is that any of us can begin to experience them right away. Not because life becomes magically perfect, but because minimalism prepares us for life. Minimalism won't solve every problem, but it will give us the space to breathe, the clarity to focus, and the freedom to build something meaningful. One step at a time, one drawer at a time, one room at a time, and one day at a time. [Music]

- Can Art Really Soothe Anxiety? Hint: YES! | 10-minute Creative Meditation with an Art Therapist: Kind: captions Language: en Take a moment to let yourself settle. My name is Ashley from Discover Art Therapy. This is your 10-minute guided creative meditation where we will release anxiety by coming into presence and connection to ourselves. Before we start, take a minute to slow your breath down and remind yourself that we are not making perfect or pretty art. We are letting the creative process flow through us and bring us into a calmer state than when we began. Begin by securing your page with a gentle tape like masking tape. Slowly and mindfully tape down all of the corners of your page. When you're ready, moisten your watercolors. This is called activating or bringing your paints to life when we add a little bit of water to each of the dry pucks. Continue to move slowly and mindfully. Preparing our materials is just as important as the meditation itself. Oftentimes with anxiety, we have so much energy in our body and it's racing up in our heads causing unnecessary anxious intense thoughts. It can be caused by so many different challenging emotions, but we're not here to change or fix those things today. Instead, this art process is going to focus on doing one mindful thing at a time. Anxiety thrives in chaos. So, let's simplify. Grab your first paintbrush. It can be any size, any shape, and dip it in your water. Start with any color that your heart desires. With active intention, let's dip our brush into our paint and get lots of bright color on it. And at the top corner of our page, we're going to create a slow and mindful circle. It doesn't have to be perfect. It doesn't have to have even thickness. You can leave your circle hollow or you can fill it in whenever you'd like. But all we're going to do after that is repeat our circle on the page. Continue to move slowly. If you've been struggling with anxiety lately, it might be hard to slow down at first. But I encourage you to stay with the process. Continue to work down your page and make the same mark over and over. It doesn't matter if they're equal. It doesn't matter if one's a little bit lopsided. We just want to focus on presence in the moment. We're stealing the microphone from the anxious thoughts up in our head and bringing our attention into mindful movements of the body. Doing one repetitive mark allows our brain to feel calm because it knows what to predict. So, keep going with that predictable, repeatable, repetitive pattern of the circle across your page and change colors when you would like to. >> Mhm. >> This is your invitation to put any final touches on your arch. You may leave your piece taped down to let it dry or begin to mindfully peel off your tape gently so it doesn't rip. And we're going to take a moment for some active reflection. How are you feeling in your mind, body, and spirit after giving yourself some space to create? After giving yourself some space to calm down. After giving yourself space to simplify. You now have a visual reminder of what it's like to come back to center. You can return to this exercise anytime that you feel anxiety or you feel away from your center. I hope this helps you feel better. Take good care of yourself and I'll see you in the next creative meditation.
